2.563	surprised, and driven in tumultuous flight,
2.564	now start to view. Full well they now can see
2.565	our shields and borrowed arms, and clearly note
2.566	our speech of alien sound; their multitude
2.567	o'erwhelms us utterly. Coroebus first
2.568	at mailed Minerva's altar prostrate lay,
2.569	pierced by Peneleus, blade; then Rhipeus fell;
2.570	we deemed him of all Trojans the most just,
2.571	most scrupulously righteous; but the gods
2.572	gave judgment otherwise. There Dymas died,
2.573	and Hypanis, by their compatriots slain;
2.574	nor thee, O Panthus, in that mortal hour,
2.575	could thy clean hands or Phoebus, priesthood save.
2.576	O ashes of my country! funeral pyre
2.577	of all my kin! bear witness that my breast
2.578	shrank not from any sword the Grecian drew,
2.579	and that my deeds the night my country died
2.580	deserved a warrior's death, had Fate ordained.
2.581	But soon our ranks were broken; at my side
2.582	stayed Iphitus and Pelias; one with age
2.583	was Iong since wearied, and the other bore
2.584	the burden of Ulysses' crippling wound.
2.585	Straightway the roar and tumult summoned us
2.586	to Priam's palace,where a battle raged
2.587	as if save this no conflict else were known,
2.588	and all Troy's dying brave were mustered there.
2.589	There we beheld the war-god unconfined;
2.590	The Greek besiegers to the roof-tops fled;
2.591	or, with shields tortoise-back, the gates assailed.
2.592	Ladders were on the walls; and round by round,
2.593	up the huge bulwark as they fight their way,
2.594	the shielded left-hand thwarts the falling spears,
2.595	the right to every vantage closely clings.
2.596	The Trojans hurl whole towers and roof-tops down
2.597	upon the mounting foe; for well they see
2.598	that the last hour is come, and with what arms
2.599	the dying must resist. Rich gilded beams,
2.600	with many a beauteous blazon of old time,
2.601	go crashing down. Men armed with naked swords
2.602	defend the inner doors in close array.
2.603	Thus were our hearts inflamed to stand and strike
2.604	for the king's house, and to his body-guard
2.605	bring succor, and renew their vanquished powers.
2.606	A certain gate I knew, a secret way,
2.607	which gave free passage between Priam's halls,
2.608	and exit rearward; hither, in the days
2.609	before our fall, the lone Andromache
2.610	was wont with young Astyanax to pass
2.611	in quest of Priam and her husband's kin.
2.612	This way to climb the palace roof I flew,
2.613	where, desperate, the Trojans with vain skill
2.614	hurled forth repellent arms. A tower was there,
2.615	reared skyward from the roof-top, giving view
2.616	of Troy's wide walls and full reconnaissance
2.617	of all Achaea's fleets and tented field;
2.618	this, with strong steel, our gathered strength assailed,
2.619	and as the loosened courses offered us
2.620	great threatening fissures, we uprooted it
2.621	from its aerial throne and thrust it down.
2.622	It fell with instantaneous crash of thunder
2.623	along the Danaan host in ruin wide.
2.624	But fresh ranks soon arrive; thick showers of stone
2.625	rain down, with every missile rage can find.
2.626	Now at the threshold of the outer court
2.627	Pyrrhus triumphant stood, with glittering arms
2.628	and helm of burnished brass. He glittered like
2.629	some swollen viper, fed on poison-leaves,
2.630	whom chilling winter shelters underground,
2.631	till, fresh and strong, he sheds his annual scales
2.632	and, crawling forth rejuvenate, uncoils
2.633	his slimy length; his lifted gorge insults
2.634	the sunbeam with three-forked and quivering tongue.
2.635	Huge Periphas was there; Automedon,
2.636	who drove Achilles' steeds, and bore his arms.
2.637	Then Scyros' island-warriors assault
2.638	the palaces, and hurl reiterate fire
2.639	at wall and tower. Pyrrhus led the van;
2.640	seizing an axe he clove the ponderous doors
2.641	and rent the hinges from their posts of bronze;
2.642	he cut the beams, and through the solid mass
2.643	burrowed his way, till like a window huge
2.644	the breach yawned wide, and opened to his gaze
2.645	a vista of long courts and corridors,
2.646	the hearth and home of many an ancient king,
2.647	and Priam's own; upon its sacred bourne
2.648	the sentry, all in arms, kept watch and ward.
2.649	Confusion, groans, and piteous turmoil
2.650	were in that dwelling; women shrieked and wailed
2.651	from many a dark retreat, and their loud cry
2.652	rang to the golden stars. Through those vast halls
2.653	the panic-stricken mothers wildly roved,
2.654	and clung with frantic kisses and embrace
2.655	unto the columns cold. Fierce as his sire,
2.656	Pyrrhus moves on; nor bar nor sentinel
2.657	may stop his way; down tumbles the great door
2.658	beneath the battering beam, and with it fall
2.659	hinges and framework violently torn.
2.660	Force bursts all bars; th' assailing Greeks break in,
2.661	do butchery, and with men-at-arms possess
2.662	what place they will. Scarce with an equal rage
2.663	a foaming river, when its dykes are down,
2.664	o'erwhelms its mounded shores, and through the plain
2.665	rolls mountain-high, while from the ravaged farms
2.666	its fierce flood sweeps along both flock and fold.
2.667	My own eyes looked on Neoptolemus
2.668	frenzied with slaughter, and both Atreus' sons
2.669	upon the threshold frowning; I beheld
2.670	her hundred daughters with old Hecuba;
2.671	and Priam, whose own bleeding wounds defiled
2.672	the altars where himself had blessed the fires;
2.673	there fifty nuptial beds gave promise proud
2.674	of princely heirs; but all their brightness now,
2.675	of broidered cunning and barbaric gold,
2.676	lay strewn and trampled on. The Danaan foe
2.677	stood victor, where the raging flame had failed.
2.678	But would ye haply know what stroke of doom
2.679	on Priam fell? Now when his anguish saw
2.680	his kingdom lost and fallen, his abode
2.681	shattered, and in his very hearth and home
2.682	th' exulting foe, the aged King did bind
2.683	his rusted armor to his trembling thews,—
2.684	all vainly,— and a useless blade of steel
2.685	he girded on; then charged, resolved to die
2.686	encircled by the foe. Within his walls
2.687	there stood, beneath the wide and open sky,
2.688	a lofty altar; an old laurel-tree
2.689	leaned o'er it, and enclasped in holy shade
2.690	the statues of the tutelary powers.
2.691	Here Hecuba and all the princesses
2.692	took refuge vain within the place of prayer.
2.693	Like panic-stricken doves in some dark storm,
2.694	close-gathering they sate, and in despair
2.695	embraced their graven gods. But when the Queen
2.696	saw Priam with his youthful harness on,
2.697	“What frenzy, O my wretched lord,” she cried,
2.698	“Arrayed thee in such arms? O, whither now?
2.699	Not such defences, nor such arm as thine,
2.700	the time requires, though thy companion were
2.701	our Hector's self. O, yield thee, I implore!
2.702	This altar now shall save us one and all,
2.703	or we must die together.” With these words
2.704	she drew him to her side, and near the shrine
2.705	made for her aged spouse a place to cling.
2.706	But, lo! just 'scaped of Pyrrhus' murderous hand,
2.707	Polites, one of Priam's sons, fled fast
2.708	along the corridors, through thronging foes
2.709	and a thick rain of spears. Wildly he gazed
2.710	across the desolate halls, wounded to death.
2.711	Fierce Pyrrhus followed after, pressing hard
2.712	with mortal stroke, and now his hand and spear
2.713	were close upon:— when the lost youth leaped forth
2.714	into his father's sight, and prostrate there
2.715	lay dying, while his life-blood ebbed away.
2.716	Then Priam, though on all sides death was nigh,
2.717	quit not the strife, nor from loud wrath refrained:
2.718	“Thy crime and impious outrage, may the gods
2.719	(if Heaven to mortals render debt and due)
2.720	justly reward and worthy honors pay!
2.721	My own son's murder thou hast made me see,
2.722	blood and pollution impiously throwing
2.723	upon a father's head. Not such was he,
2.724	not such, Achilles, thy pretended sire,
2.725	when Priam was his foe. With flush of shame
2.726	he nobly listened to a suppliant's plea
2.727	in honor made. He rendered to the tomb
2.728	my Hector's body pale, and me did send
2.729	back to my throne a king.” With this proud word
2.730	the aged warrior hurled with nerveless arm
2.731	his ineffectual spear, which hoarsely rang
2.732	rebounding on the brazen shield, and hung
2.733	piercing the midmost boss,- but all in vain.
2.734	Then Pyrrhus: “Take these tidings, and convey
2.735	message to my father, Peleus' son!
2.736	tell him my naughty deeds! Be sure and say
2.737	how Neoptolemus hath shamed his sires.
2.738	Now die!” With this, he trailed before the shrines
2.739	the trembling King, whose feet slipped in the stream
2.740	of his son's blood. Then Pyrrhus' left hand clutched
2.741	the tresses old and gray; a glittering sword
2.742	his right hand lifted high, and buried it
2.743	far as the hilt in that defenceless heart.
2.744	So Priam's story ceased. Such final doom
2.745	fell on him, while his dying eyes surveyed
2.746	Troy burning, and her altars overthrown,
2.747	though once of many an orient land and tribe
2.748	the boasted lord. In huge dismemberment
2.749	his severed trunk lies tombless on the shore,
2.750	the head from shoulder torn, the corpse unknown.
2.751	Then first wild horror on my spirit fell
2.752	and dazed me utterly. A vision rose
2.753	of my own cherished father, as I saw
2.754	the King, his aged peer, sore wounded Iying
2.755	in mortal agony; a vision too
2.756	of lost Creusa at my ravaged hearth,
2.757	and young Iulus' peril. Then my eyes
2.758	looked round me seeking aid. But all were fled,
2.759	war-wearied and undone; some earthward leaped
2.760	from battlement or tower; some in despair
2.761	yielded their suffering bodies to the flame.
2.762	I stood there sole surviving; when, behold,
2.763	to Vesta's altar clinging in dumb fear,
2.764	hiding and crouching in the hallowed shade,
2.765	Tyndarus' daughter!— 't was the burning town
2.766	lighted full well my roving steps and eyes.
2.767	In fear was she both of some Trojan's rage
2.768	for Troy o'erthrown, and of some Greek revenge,
2.769	or her wronged husband's Iong indignant ire.
2.770	So hid she at that shrine her hateful brow,
2.771	being of Greece and Troy, full well she knew,
2.772	the common curse. Then in my bosom rose
2.773	a blaze of wrath; methought I should avenge
2.774	my dying country, and with horrid deed
2.775	pay crime for crime. “Shall she return unscathed
2.776	to Sparta, to Mycenae's golden pride,
2.777	and have a royal triumph? Shall her eyes
2.778	her sire and sons, her hearth and husband see,
2.779	while Phrygian captives follow in her train?
2.780	is Priam murdered? Have the flames swept o'er
2.781	my native Troy? and cloth our Dardan strand
2.782	sweat o'er and o'er with sanguinary dew?
2.783	O, not thus unavenged! For though there be
2.784	no glory if I smite a woman's crime,
2.785	nor conqueror's fame for such a victory won,
2.786	yet if I blot this monster out, and wring
2.787	full punishment from guilt, the time to come
2.788	will praise me, and sweet pleasure it will be
2.789	to glut my soul with vengeance and appease
2.790	the ashes of my kindred.”So I raved,
2.791	and to such frenzied purpose gave my soul.
2.792	Then with clear vision (never had I seen
2.793	her presence so unclouded) I beheld,
2.794	in golden beams that pierced the midnight gloom,
2.795	my gracious mother, visibly divine,
2.796	and with that mien of majesty she wears
2.797	when seen in heaven; she stayed me with her hand,
2.798	and from her lips of rose this counsel gave:
2.799	“O son, what sorrow stirs thy boundless rage?
2.800	what madness this? Or whither vanisheth
2.801	thy love of me? Wilt thou not seek to know
2.802	where bides Anchises, thy abandoned sire,
2.803	now weak with age? or if Creusa lives
2.804	and young Ascanius, who are ringed about
2.805	with ranks of Grecian foes, and long ere this—
2.806	save that my love can shield them and defend—
2.807	had fallen on flame or fed some hungry sword?
2.808	Not Helen's hated beauty works thee woe;
2.809	nor Paris, oft-accused. The cruelty
2.810	of gods, of gods unaided, overwhelms
2.811	thy country's power, and from its Iofty height
2.812	casts Ilium down. Behold, I take away
2.813	the barrier-cloud that dims thy mortal eye,
2.814	with murk and mist o'er-veiling. Fear not thou
2.815	to heed thy mother's word, nor let thy heart
2.816	refuse obedience to her counsel given.
2.817	'Mid yonder trembling ruins, where thou see'st
2.818	stone torn from stone, with dust and smoke uprolling,
2.819	't is Neptune strikes the wall; his trident vast
2.820	makes her foundation tremble, and unseats
2.821	the city from her throne. Fierce Juno leads
2.822	resistless onset at the Scaean gate,
2.823	and summons from the ships the league of powers,
2.824	wearing her wrathful sword. On yonder height
2.825	behold Tritonia in the citadel
2.826	clothed with the lightning and her Gorgon-shield!
2.827	Unto the Greeks great Jove himself renews
2.828	their courage and their power; 't is he thrusts on
2.829	the gods themselves against the Trojan arms.
2.830	Fly, O my son! The war's wild work give o'er!
2.831	I will be always nigh and set thee safe
2.832	upon thy father's threshold.” Having said,
2.833	she fled upon the viewless night away.
2.834	Then loomed o'er Troy the apparition vast
2.835	of her dread foes divine; I seemed to see
2.836	all Ilium sink in fire, and sacred Troy,
2.837	of Neptune's building, utterly o'erthrown.
2.838	So some huge ash-tree on the mountain's brow
2.839	(when rival woodmen, heaving stroke on stroke
2.840	of two-edged axes, haste to cast her down)
2.841	sways ominously her trembling, leafy top,
2.842	and drops her smitten head; till by her wounds
2.843	vanquished at last, she makes her dying groan,
2.844	and falls in loud wreck from the cliffs uptorn.
2.845	I left the citadel; and, led by Heaven,
2.846	threaded the maze of deadly foes and fires,
2.847	through spears that glanced aside and flames that fell.
2.848	Soon came I to my father's ancient seat,
2.849	our home and heritage. But lo! my sire
2.850	(whom first of all I sought, and first would bear
2.851	to safe asylum in the distant hills)
2.852	vowed he could never, after fallen Troy,
2.853	live longer on, or bear an exile's woe.
2.854	“O you,” he cried, “whose blood not yet betrays
2.855	the cruel taint of time, whose powers be still
2.856	unpropped and undecayed, go, take your flight.
2.857	If heavenly wrath had willed my life to spare,
2.858	this dwelling had been safe. It is too much
2.859	that I have watched one wreck, and for too Iong
2.860	outlived my vanquished country. Thus, O, thus!
2.861	Compose these limbs for death, and say farewell.
2.862	My own hand will procure it; or my foe
2.863	will end me of mere pity, and for spoil
2.864	will strip me bare. It is an easy loss
2.865	to have no grave. For many a year gone by,
2.866	accursed of Heaven, I tarry in this world
2.867	a useless burden, since that fatal hour
2.868	when Jove, of gods the Sire and men the King,
2.869	his lightnings o'er me breathed and blasting fire.”
2.870	Such fixed resolve he uttered o'er and o'er,
2.871	and would not yield, though with my tears did join
2.872	my spouse Creusa, fair Ascanius,
2.873	and our whole house, imploring the gray sire
2.874	not with himself to ruin all, nor add
2.875	yet heavier burdens to our crushing doom.
2.876	He still cried, “No!” and clung to where he sat
2.877	and to the same dread purpose. I once more
2.878	back to the fight would speed. For death alone
2.879	I made my wretched prayer. What space was left
2.880	for wisdom now? What chance or hope was given?
2.881	“Didst thou, dear father, dream that I could fly
2.882	sundered from thee? Did such an infamy
2.883	fall from a father's lips? If Heaven's decree
2.884	will of this mighty nation not let live
2.885	a single soul, if thine own purpose be
2.886	to cast thyself and thy posterity
2.887	into thy country's grave, behold, the door
2.888	is open to thy death! Lo, Pyrrhus comes
2.889	red-handed from King Priam! He has slain
2.890	a son before a father's eyes, and spilt
2.891	a father's blood upon his own hearthstone.
2.892	Was it for this, O heavenly mother mine,
2.893	that thou hast brought me safe through sword and fire?
2.894	that I might see these altars desecrate
2.895	by their worst foes? that I might look upon
2.896	my sire, my wife, and sweet Ascanius
2.897	dead at my feet in one another's blood?
2.898	To arms, my men, to arms! The hour of death
2.899	now beckons to the vanquished. Let me go
2.900	whither the Greeks are gathered; let me stand
2.901	where oft revives the flagging stroke of war:
2.902	Not all of us die unavenged this day!”
2.903	I clasped my sword-belt round me once again,
2.904	fitted my left arm to my shield, and turned
2.905	to fly the house; but at the threshold clung
2.906	Creusa to my knees, and lifted up
2.907	Iulus to his father's arms. “If thou
2.908	wouldst rush on death,” she cried, “O, suffer us
2.909	to share thy perils with thee to the end.
2.910	But if this day's work bid thee trust a sword,
2.911	defend thy hearthstone first. Who else shall guard
2.912	thy babe Iulus, or thy reverend sire?
2.913	Or me, thy wife that was—what help have I?”
2.914	So rang the roof-top with her piteous cries:
2.915	but lo! a portent wonderful to see
2.916	on sudden rose; for while his parents' grief
2.917	held the boy close in arm and full in view,
2.918	there seemed upon Iulus' head to glow
2.919	a flickering peak of fire; the tongue of flame
2.920	innocuous o'er his clustering tresses played,
2.921	and hovered round his brows. We, horror-struck,
2.922	grasped at his burning hair, and sprinkled him,
2.923	to quench that holy and auspicious fire.
2.924	then sire Anchises with exultant eyes
2.925	looked heavenward, and lifted to the stars
2.926	his voice and outstretched hands. “Almighty Jove,
2.927	if aught of prayer may move thee, let thy grace
2.928	now visit us! O, hear this holy vow!
2.929	And if for service at thine altars done,
2.930	we aught can claim, O Father, lend us aid,
2.931	and ratify the omen thou hast given!”
2.932	Scarce ceased his aged voice, when suddenly
2.933	from leftward, with a deafening thunder-peal,
2.934	cleaving the blackness of the vaulted sky,
2.935	a meteor-star in trailing splendor ran,
2.936	exceeding bright. We watched it glide sublime
2.937	o'er tower and town, until its radiant beam
2.938	in forest-mantled Ida died away;
2.939	but left a furrow on its track in air,
2.940	a glittering, Iong line, while far and wide
2.941	the sulphurous fume and exhalation flowed.
2.942	My father strove not now; but lifted him
2.943	in prayer to all the gods, in holy awe
2.944	of that auspicious star, and thus exclaimed:
2.945	“Tarry no moment more! Behold, I come!
2.946	Whithersoe'er ye lead, my steps obey.
2.947	Gods of my fathers, O, preserve our name!
2.948	Preserve my son, and his! This augury
2.949	is yours; and Troy on your sole strength relies.
2.950	I yield, dear son; I journey at thy side.”
2.951	He spoke; and higher o'er the blazing walls
2.952	leaped the loud fire, while ever nearer drew
2.953	the rolling surges of tumultuous flame.
2.954	“Haste, father, on these bending shoulders climb!
2.955	This back is ready, and the burden light;
2.956	one peril smites us both, whate'er befall;
2.957	one rescue both shall find. Close at my side
2.958	let young Iulus run, while, not too nigh,
2.959	my wife Creusa heeds what way we go.
2.960	Ye servants of our house, give ear, I pray,
2.961	to my command. Outside the city's gates
2.962	lies a low mound and long since ruined fane
2.963	to Ceres vowed; a cypress, ancient shade
2.964	o'erhangs it, which our fathers' pious care
2.965	protected year by year; by various paths
2.966	be that our meeting-place. But in thy hands
2.967	bring, sire, our household gods, and sanctifies:
2.968	for me to touch, who come this very hour
2.969	from battle and the fresh blood of the slain,
2.970	were but abomination, till what time
2.971	in living waters I shall make me clean.”
2.972	So saying, I bowed my neck and shoulders broad,
2.973	o'erspread me with a lion's tawny skin,
2.974	and lifted up my load. Close at my side
2.975	little Iulus twined his hand in mine
2.976	and followed, with unequal step, his sire.
2.977	My wife at distance came. We hastened on,
2.978	creeping through shadows; I, who once had viewed
2.979	undaunted every instrument of war
2.980	and all the gathered Greeks in grim array,
2.981	now shook at every gust, and heard all sounds
2.982	with fevered trepidation, fearing both
2.983	for him I bore and him who clasped my hand.
2.984	Now near the gates I drew, and deemed our flight
2.985	safely at end, when suddenly I heard
2.986	the sounding tread of many warriors
2.987	that seemed hard-by, while through the murky night
2.988	my father peered, and shouted, “O my son,
2.989	away, away! for surely all our foes
2.990	are here upon us, and my eyes behold
2.991	the glance of glittering shields and flash of arms.”
2.992	O, then some evil-working, nameless god
2.993	clouded my senses quite: for while I sped
2.994	along our pathless way, and left behind
2.995	all paths and regions known—O wretched me!—
2.996	Creusa on some dark disaster fell;
2.997	she stopped, or wandered, or sank down undone,—
2.998	I never knew what way,—and nevermore
2.999	I looked on her alive. Yet knew I not
2.1000	my loss, nor backward turned a look or thought,
2.1001	till by that hallowed hill to Ceres vowed
2.1002	we gathered all,— and she alone came not,
2.1003	while husband, friends, and son made search in vain.
2.1004	What god, what man, did not my grief accuse
2.1005	in frenzied word? In all the ruined land
2.1006	what worse woe had I seen? Entrusting then
2.1007	my sire, my son, and all the Teucrian gods
2.1008	to the deep shadows of a slanting vale
2.1009	where my allies kept guard, I tried me back
2.1010	to that doomed town, re-girt in glittering arms.
2.1011	Resolved was I all hazards to renew,
2.1012	all Troy to re-explore, and once again
2.1013	offer my life to perils without end.
2.1014	The walls and gloomy gates whence forth I came
2.1015	I first revisit, and retrace my way,
2.1016	searching the night once more. On all sides round
2.1017	horror spread wide; the very silence breathed
2.1018	a terror on my soul. I hastened then
2.1019	back to my fallen home, if haply there
2.1020	her feet had strayed; but the invading Greeks
2.1021	were its possessors, though the hungry fire
2.1022	was blown along the roof-tree, and the flames
2.1023	rolled raging upward on the fitful gale.
2.1024	To Priam's house I haste, and climb once more
2.1025	the citadel; in Juno's temple there,
2.1026	the chosen guardians of her wasted halls,
2.1027	Phoenix and dread Ulysses watched the spoil.
2.1028	Here, snatched away from many a burning fane,
2.1029	Troy's treasures lay,—rich tables for the gods,
2.1030	thick bowls of messy gold, and vestures rare,
2.1031	confusedly heaped up, while round the pile
2.1032	fair youths and trembling virgins stood forlorn.
2.1033	Yet oft my voice rang dauntless through the gloom,
2.1034	from street to street I cried with anguish vain;
2.1035	and on Creusa piteously calling,
2.1036	woke the lamenting echoes o'er and o'er.
2.1037	While on this quest I roamed the city through,
2.1038	of reason reft there rose upon my sight—
2.1039	O shape of sorrow!— my Creusa's ghost,
2.1040	hers truly, though a loftier port it wore.
2.1041	I quailed, my hair rose, and I gasped for fear;
2.1042	but thus she spoke, and soothed my grief away:
2.1043	“Why to these frenzied sorrows bend thy soul,
2.1044	O husband ever dear! The will of Heaven
2.1045	hath brought all this to pass. Fate doth not send
2.1046	Creusa the long journeys thou shalt take,
2.1047	or hath th' Olympian King so given decree.
2.1048	Long is thy banishment; thy ship must plough
2.1049	the vast, far-spreading sea. Then shalt thou come
2.1050	unto Hesperia, whose fruitful plains
2.1051	are watered by the Tiber, Lydian stream,
2.1052	of smooth, benignant Bow. Thou shalt obtain
2.1053	fair fortunes, and a throne and royal bride.
2.1054	For thy beloved Creusa weep no more!
2.1055	No Myrmidon's proud palace waits me now;
2.1056	Dolopian shall not scorn, nor Argive dames
2.1057	command a slave of Dardan's royal stem
2.1058	and wife to Venus' son. On these loved shores
2.1059	the Mother of the Gods compels my stay.
2.1060	Farewell! farewell! O, cherish evermore
2.1061	thy son and mine!” Her utterance scarce had ceased,
2.1062	when, as I strove through tears to make reply,
2.1063	she left me, and dissolved in empty air.
2.1064	Thrice would my frustrate arms her form enfold;
2.1065	thrice from the clasp of hand that vision fled,
2.1066	like wafted winds and like a fleeting dream.
2.1067	The night had passed, and to my friends once more
2.1068	I made my way, much wondering to find
2.1069	a mighty multitude assembled there
2.1070	of friends new-come,—matrons and men-at-arms,
2.1071	and youth for exile bound,— a doleful throng.
2.1072	From far and near they drew, their hearts prepared
2.1073	and their possessions gathered, to sail forth
2.1074	to lands unknown, wherever o'er the wave
2.1075	I bade them follow. Now above the crest
2.1076	of loftiest Ida rose the morning-star,
2.1077	chief in the front of day. The Greeks held fast
2.1078	the captive gates of Troy. No help or hope
2.1079	was ours any more. Then, yielding all,
2.1080	and lifting once again my aged sire,
2.1081	for refuge to the distant hills I fled.
3.1	When Asia's power and Priam's race and throne,
3.2	though guiltless, were cast down by Heaven's decree,
3.3	when Ilium proud had fallen, and Neptune's Troy
3.4	in smouldering ash lay level with the ground,
3.5	to wandering exile then and regions wild
3.6	the gods by many an augury and sign
3.7	compelled us forth. We fashioned us a fleet
3.8	within Antander's haven, in the shade
3.9	of Phrygian Ida's peak (though knowing not
3.10	whither our fate would drive, or where afford
3.11	a resting-place at last), and my small band
3.12	of warriors I arrayed. As soon as smiled
3.13	the light of summer's prime, my reverend sire
3.14	Anchises bade us on the winds of Fate
3.15	to spread all sail. Through tears I saw recede
3.16	my native shore, the haven and the plains
3.17	where once was Troy. An exile on the seas,
3.18	with son and followers and household shrines,
3.19	and Troy's great guardian-gods, I took my way.
3.20	There is a far-off land where warriors breed,
3.21	where Thracians till the boundless plains, and where
3.22	the cruel-eyed Lycurgus once was king.
3.23	Troy's old ally it was, its deities
3.24	had brotherhood with ours before our fall.
3.25	Thither I fared, and on its winding shores
3.26	set my first walls, though partial Fate opposed
3.27	our entrance there. In memory of my name
3.28	I called its people the Aeneadae.
3.29	Unto Dione's daughter, and all gods
3.30	who blessed our young emprise, due gifts were paid;
3.31	and unto the supreme celestial King
3.32	I slew a fair white bull beside the sea.
3.33	But haply near my place of sacrifice
3.34	a mound was seen, and on the summit grew
3.35	a copse of corner and a myrtle tree,
3.36	with spear-like limbs outbranched on every side.
3.37	This I approached, and tried to rend away
3.38	from its deep roots that grove of gloomy green,
3.39	and dress my altars in its leafy boughs.
3.40	But, horrible to tell, a prodigy
3.41	smote my astonished eyes: for the first tree,
3.42	which from the earth with broken roots I drew,
3.43	dripped black with bloody drops, and gave the ground
3.44	dark stains of gore. Cold horror shook my frame,
3.45	and every vein within me froze for fear.
3.46	Once more I tried from yet another stock
3.47	the pliant stem to tear, and to explore
3.48	the mystery within,—but yet again
3.49	the foul bark oozed with clots of blackest gore!
3.50	From my deep-shaken soul I made a prayer
3.51	to all the woodland nymphs and to divine
3.52	Gradivus, patron of the Thracian plain,
3.53	to bless this sight, to lift its curse away.
3.54	But when at a third sheaf of myrtle spears
3.55	I fell upon my knees, and tugged amain
3.56	against the adverse ground (I dread to tell!),
3.57	a moaning and a wail from that deep grave
3.58	burst forth and murmured in my listening ear:
3.59	“Why wound me, great Aeneas, in my woe?
3.60	O, spare the dead, nor let thy holy hands
3.61	do sacrilege and sin! I, Trojan-born,
3.62	was kin of thine. This blood is not of trees.
3.63	Haste from this murderous shore, this land of greed.
3.64	O, I am Polydorus! Haste away!
3.65	Here was I pierced; a crop of iron spears
3.66	has grown up o'er my breast, and multiplied
3.67	to all these deadly javelins, keen and strong.”
3.68	Then stood I, burdened with dark doubt and fear
3.69	I quailed, my hair rose and my utterance choked.
3.70	For once this Polydorus, with much gold,
3.71	ill-fated Priam sent by stealth away
3.72	for nurture with the Thracian king, what time
3.73	Dardania's war Iooked hopeless, and her towers
3.74	were ringed about by unrelenting siege.
3.75	That king, when Ilium's cause was ebbing low,
3.76	and fortune frowned, gave o'er his plighted faith
3.77	to Agamemnon's might and victory;
3.78	he scorned all honor and did murder foul
3.79	on Polydorus, seizing lawlessly
3.80	on all the gold. O, whither at thy will,
3.81	curst greed of gold, may mortal hearts be driven?
3.82	Soon as my shuddering ceased, I told this tale
3.83	of prodigies before the people's chiefs,
3.84	who sat in conclave with my kingly sire,
3.85	and bade them speak their reverend counsel forth.
3.86	All found one voice; to leave that land of sin,
3.87	where foul abomination had profaned
3.88	a stranger's right; and once more to resign
3.89	our fleet unto the tempest and the wave.
3.90	But fit and solemn funeral rites were paid
3.91	to Polydorus. A high mound we reared
3.92	of heaped-up earth, and to his honored shade
3.93	built a perpetual altar, sadly dressed
3.94	in cypress dark and purple pall of woe.
3.95	Our Ilian women wailed with loosened hair;
3.96	new milk was sprinkled from a foaming cup,
3.97	and from the shallow bowl fresh blood out-poured
3.98	upon the sacred ground. So in its tomb
3.99	we laid his ghost to rest, and loudly sang,
3.100	with prayer for peace, the long, the last farewell.
3.101	After these things, when first the friendly sea
3.102	looked safe and fair, and o'er its tranquil plain
3.103	light-whispering breezes bade us launch away,
3.104	my men drew down our galleys to the brine,
3.105	thronging the shore. Soon out of port we ran,
3.106	and watched the hills and cities fading far.
3.107	There is a sacred island in mid-seas,
3.108	to fruitful Doris and to Neptune dear,
3.109	which grateful Phoebus, wielder of the bow,
3.110	the while it drifted loose from land to land,
3.111	chained firmly where the crags of Gyaros
3.112	and Myconos uptower, and bade it rest
3.113	immovable, in scorn of wind and wave.
3.114	Thither I sped; by this my weary ships
3.115	found undisturbed retreat and haven fair.
3.116	To land we came and saw with reverent eyes
3.117	Apollo's citadel. King Anius,
3.118	his people's king, and priest at Phoebus' fane,
3.119	came forth to meet us, wearing on his brow
3.120	the fillets and a holy laurel crown.
3.121	Unto Anchises he gave greeting kind,
3.122	claimed old acquaintance, grasped us by the hand,
3.123	and bade us both his roof and welcome share.
3.124	Then, kneeling at the shrine of time-worn stone:
3.125	“Thou who at Thymbra on the Trojan shore
3.126	hast often blessed my prayer, O, give to me
3.127	a hearth and home, and to this war-worn band
3.128	defensive towers and offspring multiplied
3.129	in an abiding city; give to Troy
3.130	a second citadel, that shall survive
3.131	Achilles' wrath and all our Argive foe.
3.132	Whom shall we follow? Whither lies our way?
3.133	Where wilt thou grant us an abiding-place?
3.134	Send forth, O King, thy voice oracular,
3.135	and on our spirits move.” Scarce had I spoke
3.136	when sudden trembling through the laurels ran
3.137	and smote the holy portals; far and wide
3.138	the mighty ridges of the mountain shook,
3.139	and from the opening shrine the tripod moaned.
3.140	Prostrate to earth we fall, as on our ears
3.141	this utterance breaks: “O breed of iron men,
3.142	ye sons of Dardanus! the self-same land
3.143	where bloomed at first your far-descended stem
3.144	shall to its bounteous bosom draw ye home.
3.145	Seek out your ancient Mother! There at last
3.146	Aeneas' race shall reign on every shore,
3.147	and his sons' sons, and all their house to be.”
3.148	So Phoebus spoke; and mighty joy uprose
3.149	from all my thronging people, who would know
3.150	where Phoebus' city lay, and whitherward
3.151	the god ordained the wandering tribe's return.
3.152	Then spake my father, pondering olden days
3.153	and sacred memories of heroes gone:
3.154	“Hear, chiefs and princes, what your hopes shall be!
3.155	The Isle of Crete, abode of lofty Jove,
3.156	rests in the middle sea. Thence Ida soars;
3.157	there is the cradle of our race. It boasts
3.158	a hundred cities, seats of fruitful power.
3.159	Thence our chief sire, if duly I recall
3.160	the olden tale, King Teucer sprung, who first
3.161	touched on the Trojan shore, and chose his seat
3.162	of kingly power. There was no Ilium then
3.163	nor towered Pergama; in lowly vales
3.164	their dwelling; hence the ancient worship given
3.165	to the Protectress of Mount Cybele,
3.166	mother of Gods, what time in Ida's grove
3.167	the brazen Corybantic cymbals clang,
3.168	or sacred silence guards her mystery,
3.169	and lions yoked her royal chariot draw.
3.170	Up, then, and follow the behests divine!
3.171	Pour offering to the winds, and point your keels
3.172	unto that realm of Minos. It is near.
3.173	if Jove but bless, the third day's dawn should see
3.174	our ships at Cretan land.” So, having said,
3.175	he slew the victims for each altar's praise.
3.176	A bull to Neptune, and a bull to thee,
3.177	o beauteous Apollo! A black lamb
3.178	unto the clouds and storms; but fleece of snow
3.179	to the mild zephyrs was our offering.
3.180	The tale was told us that Idomeneus,
3.181	from his hereditary kindgom driven,
3.182	had left his Crete abandoned, that no foe
3.183	now harbored there, but all its dwellings lay
3.184	untenanted of man. So forth we sailed
3.185	out of the port of Delos, and sped far
3.186	along the main. The maenad-haunted hills
3.187	of Naxos came in view; the ridges green
3.188	of fair Donysa, with Olearos,
3.189	and Paros, gleaming white, and Cyclades
3.190	scattered among the waves, as close we ran
3.191	where thick-strewn islands vex the channelled seas
3.192	with rival shout the sailors cheerly called:
3.193	“On, comrades! On, to Crete and to our sires!”
3.194	Freely behind us blew the friendly winds,
3.195	and gave smooth passage to that fabled shore,
3.196	the land of the Curetes, friends of Jove.
3.197	There eagerly I labored at the walls
3.198	of our long-prayed-for city; and its name
3.199	was Pergamea; to my Trojan band,
3.200	pleased with such name, I gave command to build
3.201	altar and hearth, and raise the lofty tower.
3.202	But scarce the ships were beached along the strand
3.203	(While o'er the isle my busy mariners
3.204	ploughed in new fields and took them wives once more, —
3.205	I giving homes and laws) when suddenly
3.206	a pestilence from some infectious sky
3.207	seized on man's flesh, and horribly exhaled
3.208	o'er trees and crops a fatal year of plague.
3.209	Some breathed their last, while others weak and worn
3.210	lived on; the dog-star parched the barren fields;
3.211	grass withered, and the sickly, mouldering corn
3.212	refused us life. My aged father then
3.213	bade us re-cross the waves and re-implore
3.214	Apollo's mercy at his island shrine;
3.215	if haply of our weariness and woe
3.216	he might vouchsafe the end, or bid us find
3.217	help for our task, or guidance o'er the sea.
3.218	'T was night, and sleep possessed all breathing things;
3.219	when, lo! the sacred effigies divine,
3.220	the Phrygian gods which through the flames I bore
3.221	from fallen Troy, seemed in a vision clear
3.222	to stand before me where I slumbering lay,
3.223	bathed in bright beams which from the moon at full
3.224	streamed through the latticed wall: and thus they spoke
3.225	to soothe my care away. “Apollo's word,
3.226	which in far Delos the god meant for thee,
3.227	is uttered here. Behold, he sends ourselves
3.228	to this thy house, before thy prayer is made.
3.229	We from Troy's ashes have companioned thee
3.230	in every fight; and we the swollen seas,
3.231	guided by thee, in thine own ships have crossed;
3.232	our power divine shall set among the stars
3.233	thy seed to be, and to thy city give
3.234	dominion evermore. For mighty men
3.235	go build its mighty walls! Seek not to shun
3.236	the hard, long labors of an exile's way.
3.237	Change this abode! Not thine this Cretan shore,
3.238	nor here would Delian Phoebus have thee bide.
3.239	There is a land the roving Greeks have named
3.240	Hesperia. It is a storied realm
3.241	made mighty by great wars and fruitful land.
3.242	Oenotrians had it, and their sons, 't is said,
3.243	have called it Italy, a chieftain's name
3.244	to a whole region given. That land alone
3.245	our true abode can be; for Dardanus
3.246	was cradled there, and old Iasius,
3.247	their blood the oldest of our ancient line.
3.248	Arise! go forth and cheer thy father gray
3.249	with the glad tidings! Bid him doubt no more!
3.250	Ausonia seek and Corythus; for Jove
3.251	denies this Cretan realm to thine and thee.”
3.252	I marvelled at the heavenly presences
3.253	so vocal and so bright, for 't was not sleep;
3.254	but face to face I deemed I could discern
3.255	each countenance august and holy brow,
3.256	each mantled head; and from my body ran
3.257	cold sweat of awe. From my low couch I sprang,
3.258	lifting to heaven my suppliant hands and prayer,
3.259	and o'er my hearth poured forth libations free.
3.260	After th' auspicious offering, I told
3.261	Anchises the whole tale in order due.
3.262	He owned our stock two-branched, of our great sires
3.263	the twofold line, and that his thought had strayed,
3.264	in new confusion mingling ancient names;
3.265	then spoke: “O son, in Ilium's doom severe
3.266	afflicted ever! To my ears alone
3.267	this dark vicissitude Cassandra sang.
3.268	I mind me now that her wild tongue foretold
3.269	such destiny. For oft she called aloud
3.270	‘Hesperia!’ oft ‘Italia's kingdom!’ called.
3.271	But who had faith that Teucer's sons should come
3.272	to far Hesperia? What mortal ear
3.273	gave heed to sad Cassandra's voice divine?
3.274	Now Phoebus speaks. Obedient let us be,
3.275	and, warned by him, our happier Iot pursue!”
3.276	He spoke: with heart of hope we all obeyed;
3.277	again we changed abode; and, leaving there
3.278	a feeble few, again with spreading sails
3.279	we coursed in hollow ship the spacious sea.
3.280	When from the deep the shores had faded far,
3.281	and only sky and sea were round our way,
3.282	full in the zenith hung a purple cloud,
3.283	storm-laden, dark as night, and every wave
3.284	grew black and angry, while perpetual gales
3.285	came rolling o'er the main, and mountain-high
3.286	the wreckful surges rose; our ships were hurled
3.287	wide o'er the whirling waters; thunder-clouds
3.288	and misty murk of night made end of all
3.289	the light of heaven, save where the rifted storm
3.290	flashed with the oft-reiterate shaft of Jove.
3.291	Then went we drifting, beaten from our course,
3.292	upon a trackless sea. Not even the eyes
3.293	of Palinurus could tell night from noon
3.294	or ken our way. Three days of blinding dark,
3.295	three nights without a star, we roved the seas;
3.296	The fourth, land seemed to rise. Far distant hills
3.297	and rolling smoke we saw. Down came our sails,
3.298	out flew the oars, and with prompt stroke the crews
3.299	swept the dark waves and tossed the crested foam.
3.300	From such sea-peril safe, I made the shores
3.301	of Strophades,—a name the Grecians gave
3.302	to islands in the broad Ionic main, —
3.303	the Strophades, where dread Celaeno bides,
3.304	with other Harpies, who had quit the halls
3.305	of stricken Phineus, and for very fear
3.306	fled from the routed feast; no prodigy
3.307	more vile than these, nor plague more pitiless
3.308	ere rose by wrath divine from Stygian wave;
3.309	birds seem they, but with face like woman-kind;
3.310	foul-flowing bellies, hands with crooked claws,
3.311	and ghastly lips they have, with hunger pale.
3.312	Scarce had we made the haven, when, behold!
3.313	Fair herds of cattle roaming a wide plain,
3.314	and horned goats, untended, feeding free
3.315	in pastures green, surprised our happy eyes.
3.316	with eager blades we ran to take and slay,
3.317	asking of every god, and chicfly Jove,
3.318	to share the welcome prize: we ranged a feast,
3.319	with turf-built couches and a banquet-board
3.320	along the curving strand. But in a trice,
3.321	down from the high hills swooping horribly,
3.322	the Harpies loudly shrieking, flapped their wings,
3.323	snatched at our meats, and with infectious touch
3.324	polluted all; infernal was their cry,
3.325	the stench most vile. Once more in covert far
3.326	beneath a caverned rock, and close concealed
3.327	with trees and branching shade, we raised aloft
3.328	our tables, altars, and rekindled fires.
3.329	Once more from haunts unknown the clamorous flock
3.330	from every quarter flew, and seized its prey
3.331	with taloned feet and carrion lip most foul.
3.332	I called my mates to arms and opened war
3.333	on that accursed brood. My band obeyed;
3.334	and, hiding in deep grass their swords and shields,
3.335	in ambush lay. But presently the foe
3.336	swept o'er the winding shore with loud alarm :
3.337	then from a sentry-crag, Misenus blew
3.338	a signal on his hollow horn. My men
3.339	flew to the combat strange, and fain would wound
3.340	with martial steel those foul birds of the sea;
3.341	but on their sides no wounding blade could fall,
3.342	nor any plume be marred. In swiftest flight
3.343	to starry skies they soared, and left on earth
3.344	their half-gnawed, stolen feast, and footprints foul.
3.345	Celaeno only on a beetling crag
3.346	took lofty perch, and, prophetess of ill,
3.347	shrieked malediction from her vulture breast:
3.348	“Because of slaughtered kine and ravished herd,
3.349	sons of Laomedon, have ye made war?
3.350	And will ye from their rightful kingdom drive
3.351	the guiltless Harpies? Hear, O, hear my word
3.352	(Long in your bosoms may it rankle sore!)
3.353	which Jove omnipotent to Phoebus gave,
3.354	Phoebus to me: a word of doom, which I,
3.355	the Furies' elder sister, here unfold:
3.356	‘To Italy ye fare. The willing winds
3.357	your call have heard; and ye shall have your prayer
3.358	in some Italian haven safely moored.
3.359	But never shall ye rear the circling walls
3.360	of your own city, till for this our blood
3.361	by you unjustly spilt, your famished jaws
3.362	bite at your tables, aye,—and half devour.’”
3.363	She spoke: her pinions bore her to the grove,
3.364	and she was seen no more. But all my band
3.365	shuddered with shock of fear in each cold vein;
3.366	their drooping spirits trusted swords no more,
3.367	but turned to prayers and offerings, asking grace,
3.368	scarce knowing if those creatures were divine,
3.369	or but vast birds, ill-omened and unclean.
3.370	Father Anchises to the gods in heaven
3.371	uplifted suppliant hands, and on that shore
3.372	due ritual made, crying aloud; “Ye gods
3.373	avert this curse, this evil turn away!
3.374	Smile, Heaven, upon your faithful votaries.”
3.375	Then bade he launch away, the chain undo,
3.376	set every cable free and spread all sail.
3.377	O'er the white waves we flew, and took our way
3.378	where'er the helmsman or the winds could guide.
3.379	Now forest-clad Zacynthus met our gaze,
3.380	engirdled by the waves; Dulichium,
3.381	same, and Neritos, a rocky steep,
3.382	uprose. We passed the cliffs of Ithaca
3.383	that called Laertes king, and flung our curse
3.384	on fierce Ulysses' hearth and native land.
3.385	nigh hoar Leucate's clouded crest we drew,
3.386	where Phoebus' temple, feared by mariners,
3.387	loomed o'er us; thitherward we steered and reached
3.388	the little port and town. Our weary fleet
3.389	dropped anchor, and lay beached along the strand.
3.390	So, safe at land, our hopeless peril past,
3.391	we offered thanks to Jove, and kindled high
3.392	his altars with our feast and sacrifice;
3.393	then, gathering on Actium's holy shore,
3.394	made fair solemnities of pomp and game.
3.395	My youth, anointing their smooth, naked limbs,
3.396	wrestled our wonted way. For glad were we,
3.397	who past so many isles of Greece had sped
3.398	and 'scaped our circling foes. Now had the sun
3.399	rolled through the year's full circle, and the waves
3.400	were rough with icy winter's northern gales.
3.401	I hung for trophy on that temple door
3.402	a swelling shield of brass (which once was worn
3.403	by mighty Abas) graven with this line:
3.404	SPOIL OF AENEAS FROM TRIUMPHANT FOES.
3.405	Then from that haven I command them forth;
3.406	my good crews take the thwarts, smiting the sea
3.407	with rival strokes, and skim the level main.
3.408	Soon sank Phaeacia's wind-swept citadels
3.409	out of our view; we skirted the bold shores
3.410	of proud Epirus, in Chaonian land,
3.411	and made Buthrotum's port and towering town.
3.412	Here wondrous tidings met us, that the son
3.413	of Priam, Helenus, held kingly sway
3.414	o'er many Argive cities, having wed
3.415	the Queen of Pyrrhus, great Achilles' son,
3.416	and gained his throne; and that Andromache
3.417	once more was wife unto a kindred lord.
3.418	Amazement held me; all my bosom burned
3.419	to see the hero's face and hear this tale
3.420	of strange vicissitude. So up I climbed,
3.421	leaving the haven, fleet, and friendly shore.
3.422	That self-same hour outside the city walls,
3.423	within a grove where flowed the mimic stream
3.424	of a new Simois, Andromache,
3.425	with offerings to the dead, and gifts of woe,
3.426	poured forth libation, and invoked the shade
3.427	of Hector, at a tomb which her fond grief
3.428	had consecrated to perpetual tears,
3.429	though void; a mound of fair green turf it stood,
3.430	and near it rose twin altars to his name.
3.431	She saw me drawing near; our Trojan helms
3.432	met her bewildered eyes, and, terror-struck
3.433	at the portentous sight, she swooning fell
3.434	and lay cold, rigid, lifeless, till at last,
3.435	scarce finding voice, her lips addressed me thus :
3.436	“Have I true vision? Bringest thou the word
3.437	Of truth, O goddess-born? Art still in flesh?
3.438	Or if sweet light be fled, my Hector, where?”
3.439	With flood of tears she spoke, and all the grove
3.440	reechoed to her cry. Scarce could I frame
3.441	brief answer to her passion, but replied
3.442	with broken voice and accents faltering:
3.443	“I live, 't is true. I lengthen out my days
3.444	through many a desperate strait. But O, believe
3.445	that what thine eyes behold is vision true.
3.446	Alas! what lot is thine, that wert unthroned
3.447	from such a husband's side? What after-fate
3.448	could give thee honor due? Andromache,
3.449	once Hector's wife, is Pyrrhus still thy lord?”
3.450	With drooping brows and lowly voice she cried :
3.451	“O, happy only was that virgin blest,
3.452	daughter of Priam, summoned forth to die
3.453	in sight of Ilium, on a foeman's tomb!
3.454	No casting of the lot her doom decreed,
3.455	nor came she to her conqueror's couch a slave.
3.456	Myself from burning Ilium carried far
3.457	o'er seas and seas, endured the swollen pride
3.458	of that young scion of Achilles' race,
3.459	and bore him as his slave a son. When he
3.460	sued for Hermione, of Leda's line,
3.461	and nuptial-bond with Lacedaemon's Iords,
3.462	I, the slave-wife, to Helenus was given,
3.463	and slave was wed with slave. But afterward
3.464	Orestes, crazed by loss of her he loved,
3.465	and ever fury-driven from crime to crime,
3.466	crept upon Pyrrhus in a careless hour
3.467	and murdered him upon his own hearth-stone.
3.468	Part of the realm of Neoptolemus
3.469	fell thus to Helenus, who called his lands
3.470	Chaonian, and in Trojan Chaon's name
3.471	his kingdom is Chaonia. Yonder height
3.472	is Pergamus, our Ilian citadel.
3.473	What power divine did waft thee to our shore,
3.474	not knowing whither? Tell me of the boy
3.475	Ascanius! Still breathes he earthly air?
3.476	In Troy she bore him—is he mourning still
3.477	that mother ravished from his childhood's eyes?
3.478	what ancient valor stirs the manly soul
3.479	of thine own son, of Hector's sister's child?”
3.480	Thus poured she forth full many a doleful word
3.481	with unavailing tears. But as she ceased,
3.482	out of the city gates appeared the son
3.483	of Priam, Helenus, with princely train.
3.484	He welcomed us as kin, and glad at heart
3.485	gave guidance to his house, though oft his words
3.486	fell faltering and few, with many a tear.
3.487	Soon to a humbler Troy I lift my eyes,
3.488	and of a mightier Pergamus discern
3.489	the towering semblance; there a scanty stream
3.490	runs on in Xanthus' name, and my glad arms
3.491	the pillars of a Scaean gate embrace.
3.492	My Teucrian mariners with welcome free
3.493	enjoyed the friendly town; his ample halls
3.494	our royal host threw wide; full wine-cups flowed
3.495	within the palace; golden feast was spread,
3.496	and many a goblet quaffed. Day followed day,
3.497	while favoring breezes beckoned us to sea,
3.498	and swelled the waiting canvas as they blew.
3.499	Then to the prophet-priest I made this prayer:
3.500	“Offspring of Troy, interpreter of Heaven!
3.501	Who knowest Phoebus' power, and readest well
3.502	the tripod, stars, and vocal laurel leaves
3.503	to Phoebus dear, who know'st of every bird
3.504	the ominous swift wing or boding song,
3.505	o, speak! For all my course good omens showed,
3.506	and every god admonished me to sail
3.507	in quest of Italy's far-distant shores;
3.508	but lone Celaeno, heralding strange woe,
3.509	foretold prodigious horror, vengeance dark,
3.510	and vile, unnatural hunger. How elude
3.511	such perils? Or by what hard duty done
3.512	may such huge host of evils vanquished be?”
3.513	Then Helenus, with sacrifice of kine
3.514	in order due, implored the grace of Heaven,
3.515	unloosed the fillets from his sacred brow,
3.516	and led me, Phoebus, to thy temple's door,
3.517	awed by th' o'er-brooding godhead, whose true priest,
3.518	with lips inspired, made this prophetic song:
3.519	“O goddess-born, indubitably shines
3.520	the blessing of great gods upon thy path
3.521	across the sea; the heavenly King supreme
3.522	thy destiny ordains; 't is he unfolds
3.523	the grand vicissitude, which now pursues
3.524	a course immutable. I will declare
3.525	of thy large fate a certain bounded part;
3.526	that fearless thou may'st view the friendly sea,
3.527	and in Ausonia's haven at the last
3.528	find thee a fixed abode. Than this no more
3.529	the Sister Fates to Helenus unveil,
3.530	and Juno, Saturn's daughter, grants no more.
3.531	First, that Italia (which nigh at hand
3.532	thou deemest, and wouldst fondly enter in
3.533	by yonder neighboring bays) lies distant far
3.534	o'er trackless course and long, with interval
3.535	of far-extended lands. Thine oars must ply
3.536	the waves of Sicily; thy fleet must cleave
3.537	the large expanse of that Ausonian brine;
3.538	the waters of Avernus thou shalt see,
3.539	and that enchanted island where abides
3.540	Aeaean Circe, ere on tranquil shore
3.541	thou mayest plant thy nation. Lo! a sign
3.542	I tell thee; hide this wonder in thy heart:
3.543	Beside a certain stream's sequestered wave,
3.544	thy troubled eyes, in shadowy flex grove
3.545	that fringes on the river, shall descry
3.546	a milk-white, monstrous sow, with teeming brood
3.547	of thirty young, new littered, white like her,
3.548	all clustering at her teats, as prone she lies.
3.549	There is thy city's safe, predestined ground,
3.550	and there thy labors' end. Vex not thy heart
3.551	about those ‘tables bitten’, for kind fate
3.552	thy path will show, and Phoebus bless thy prayer.
3.553	But from these lands and yon Italian shore,
3.554	where from this sea of ours the tide sweeps in,
3.555	escape and flee, for all its cities hold
3.556	pernicious Greeks, thy foes: the Locri there
3.557	have builded walls; the wide Sallentine fields
3.558	are filled with soldiers of Idomeneus;
3.559	there Meliboean Philoctetes' town,
3.560	petilia, towers above its little wall.
3.561	Yea, even when thy fleet has crossed the main,
3.562	and from new altars built along the shore
3.563	thy vows to Heaven are paid, throw o'er thy head
3.564	a purple mantle, veiling well thy brows,
3.565	lest, while the sacrificial fire ascends
3.566	in offering to the gods, thine eye behold
3.567	some face of foe, and every omen fail.
3.568	Let all thy people keep this custom due,
3.569	and thou thyself be faithful; let thy seed
3.570	forever thus th' immaculate rite maintain.
3.571	After departing hence, thou shalt be blown
3.572	toward Sicily, and strait Pelorus' bounds
3.573	will open wide. Then take the leftward way:
3.574	those leftward waters in long circuit sweep,
3.575	far from that billowy coast, the opposing side.
3.576	These regions, so they tell, in ages gone
3.577	by huge and violent convulsion riven
3.578	(Such mutability is wrought by time),
3.579	sprang wide asunder; where the doubled strand
3.580	sole and continuous lay, the sea's vast power
3.581	burst in between, and bade its waves divide
3.582	Hesperia's bosom from fair Sicily,
3.583	while with a straitened firth it interflowed
3.584	their fields and cities sundered shore from shore.
3.585	The right side Scylla keeps; the left is given
3.586	to pitiless Charybdis, who draws down
3.587	to the wild whirling of her steep abyss
3.588	the monster waves, and ever and anon
3.589	flings them at heaven, to lash the tranquil stars.
3.590	But Scylla, prisoned in her eyeless cave,
3.591	thrusts forth her face, and pulls upon the rocks
3.592	ship after ship; the parts that first be seen
3.593	are human; a fair-breasted virgin she,
3.594	down to the womb; but all that lurks below
3.595	is a huge-membered fish, where strangely join
3.596	the flukes of dolphins and the paunch of wolves.
3.597	Better by far to round the distant goal
3.598	of the Trinacrian headlands, veering wide
3.599	from thy true course, than ever thou shouldst see
3.600	that shapeless Scylla in her vaulted cave,
3.601	where grim rocks echo her dark sea-dogs' roar.
3.602	Yea, more, if aught of prescience be bestowed
3.603	on Helenus, if trusted prophet he,
3.604	and Phoebus to his heart true voice have given,
3.605	o goddess-born, one counsel chief of all
3.606	I tell thee oft, and urge it o'er and o'er.
3.607	To Juno's godhead lift thy Ioudest prayer;
3.608	to Juno chant a fervent votive song,
3.609	and with obedient offering persuade
3.610	that potent Queen. So shalt thou, triumphing,
3.611	to Italy be sped, and leave behind
3.612	Trinacria.When wafted to that shore,
3.613	repair to Cumae's hill, and to the Lake
3.614	Avernus with its whispering grove divine.
3.615	There shalt thou see a frenzied prophetess,
3.616	who from beneath the hollow scarped crag
3.617	sings oracles, or characters on leaves
3.618	mysterious names. Whate'er the virgin writes,
3.619	on leaves inscribing the portentous song,
3.620	she sets in order, and conceals them well
3.621	in her deep cave, where they abide unchanged
3.622	in due array. Yet not a care has she,
3.623	if with some swinging hinge a breeze sweeps in,
3.624	to catch them as they whirl: if open door
3.625	disperse them flutterlig through the hollow rock,
3.626	she will not link their shifted sense anew,
3.627	nor re-invent her fragmentary song.
3.628	Oft her unanswered votaries depart,
3.629	scorning the Sibyl's shrine. But deem not thou
3.630	thy tarrying too Iong, whate'er thy stay.
3.631	Though thy companions chide, though winds of power
3.632	invite thy ship to sea, and well would speed
3.633	the swelling sail, yet to that Sibyl go.
3.634	Pray that her own lips may sing forth for thee
3.635	the oracles, uplifting her dread voice
3.636	in willing prophecy. Her rede shall tell
3.637	of Italy, its wars and tribes to be,
3.638	and of what way each burden and each woe
3.639	may be escaped, or borne. Her favoring aid
3.640	will grant swift, happy voyages to thy prayer.
3.641	Such counsels Heaven to my lips allows.
3.642	arise, begone! and by thy glorious deeds
3.643	set Troy among the stars! “
3.644	So spake the prophet with benignant voice.
3.645	Then gifts he bade be brought of heavy gold
3.646	and graven ivory, which to our ships
3.647	he bade us bear; each bark was Ioaded full
3.648	with messy silver and Dodona's pride
3.649	of brazen cauldrons; a cuirass he gave
3.650	of linked gold enwrought and triple chain;
3.651	a noble helmet, too, with flaming crest
3.652	and lofty cone, th' accoutrement erewhile
3.653	of Neoptolemus. My father too
3.654	had fit gifts from the King; whose bounty then
3.655	gave steeds and riders; and new gear was sent
3.656	to every sea-worn ship, while he supplied
3.657	seafarers, kit to all my loyal crews.
3.658	Anchises bade us speedily set sail,
3.659	nor lose a wind so fair; and answering him,
3.660	Apollo's priest made reverent adieu:
3.661	“Anchises, honored by the love sublime
3.662	of Venus, self and twice in safety borne
3.663	from falling Troy, chief care of kindly Heaven,
3.664	th' Ausonian shore is thine. Sail thitherward!
3.665	For thou art pre-ordained to travel far
3.666	o'er yonder seas; far in the distance lies
3.667	that region of Ausonia, Phoebus' voice
3.668	to thee made promise of. Onward, I say,
3.669	o blest in the exceeding loyal love
3.670	of thy dear son! Why keep thee longer now?
3.671	Why should my words yon gathering winds detain?”
3.672	Likewise Andromache in mournful guise
3.673	took last farewell, bringing embroidered robes
3.674	of golden woof; a princely Phrygian cloak
3.675	she gave Ascanius, vying with the King
3.676	in gifts of honor; and threw o'er the boy
3.677	the labors of her loom, with words like these:
3.678	“Accept these gifts, sweet youth, memorials
3.679	of me and my poor handicraft, to prove
3.680	th' undying friendship of Andromache,
3.681	once Hector's wife. Take these last offerings
3.682	of those who are thy kin—O thou that art
3.683	of my Astyanax in all this world
3.684	the only image! His thy lovely eyes!
3.685	Thy hands, thy lips, are even what he bore,
3.686	and like thy own his youthful bloom would be.”
3.687	Thus I made answer, turning to depart
3.688	with rising tears: “Live on, and be ye blessed,
3.689	whose greatness is accomplished! As for me,
3.690	from change to change Fate summons, and I go;
3.691	but ye have won repose. No leagues of sea
3.692	await your cleaving keel. Not yours the quest
3.693	of fading Italy's delusive shore.
3.694	Here a new Xanthus and a second Troy
3.695	your labor fashioned and your eyes may see—
3.696	more blest, I trust, less tempting to our foes!
3.697	If e'er on Tiber and its bordering vales
3.698	I safely enter, and these eyes behold
3.699	our destined walls, then in fraternal bond
3.700	let our two nations live, whose mutual boast
3.701	is one Dardanian blood, one common story.
3.702	Epirus with Hesperia shall be
3.703	one Troy in heart and soul. But this remains
3.704	for our sons' sons the happy task and care.”
3.705	Forth o'er the seas we sped and kept our course
3.706	nigh the Ceraunian headland, where begins
3.707	the short sea-passage unto Italy.
3.708	Soon sank the sun, while down the shadowed hills
3.709	stole deeper gloom; then making shore, we flung
3.710	our bodies on a dry, sea-bordering sand,
3.711	couched on earth's welcome breast; the oars were ranged
3.712	in order due; the tides of slumber dark
3.713	o'erflowed our lives. But scarce the chariot
3.714	of Night, on wings of swift, obedient Hours,
3.715	had touched the middle sky, when wakeful sprang
3.716	good Palinurus from his pillowed stone:
3.717	with hand at ear he caught each airy gust
3.718	and questioned of the winds; the gliding stars
3.719	he called by name, as onward they advanced
3.720	through the still heaven; Arcturus he beheld,
3.721	the Hyades, rain-bringers, the twin Bears,
3.722	and vast Orion girt in golden arms.
3.723	He blew a trumpet from his ship; our camp
3.724	stirred to the signal for embarking; soon
3.725	we rode the seas once more with swelling sail.
3.726	Scarce had Aurora's purple from the sky
3.727	warned off the stars, when Iying very low
3.728	along th' horizon, the dimmed hills we saw
3.729	of Italy; Achates first gave cry
3.730	“Italia!” with answering shouts of joy,
3.731	my comrades' voices cried, “Italia, hail!”
3.732	Anchises, then, wreathed a great bowl with flowers
3.733	and filled with wine, invoking Heaven to bless,
3.734	and thus he prayed from our ship's lofty stern:
3.735	“O Iords of land and sea and every storm!
3.736	Breathe favoring breezes for our onward way!”
3.737	Fresh blew the prayed-for winds. A haven fair
3.738	soon widened near us; and its heights were crowned
3.739	by a Greek fane to Pallas. Yet my men
3.740	furled sail and shoreward veered the pointing prow.
3.741	the port receding from the orient wave
3.742	is curved into a bow; on either side
3.743	the jutting headlands toss the salt sea-foam
3.744	and hide the bay itself. Like double wall
3.745	the towered crags send down protecting arms,
3.746	while distant from the shore the temple stands.
3.747	Here on a green sward, the first omen given,
3.748	I saw four horses grazing through the field,
3.749	each white as snow. Father Anchises cried:
3.750	“Is war thy gift, O new and alien land?
3.751	Horses make war; of war these creatures bode.
3.752	Yet oft before the chariot of peace
3.753	their swift hoofs go, and on their necks they bear
3.754	th' obedient yoke and rein. Therefore a hope
3.755	of peace is also ours.” Then we implored
3.756	Minerva's mercy, at her sacred shrine,
3.757	the mail-clad goddess who gave welcome there;
3.758	and at an altar, mantling well our brows
3.759	the Phrygian way, as Helenus ordained,
3.760	we paid the honors his chief counsel urged,
3.761	with blameless rite, to Juno, Argive Queen.
3.762	No tarrying now, but after sacrifice
3.763	we twirled the sailyards and shook out all sail,
3.764	leaving the cities of the sons of Greece
3.765	and that distrusted land. Tarentum's bay
3.766	soon smiled before us, town of Hercules,
3.767	if fame be true; opposing it uptowers
3.768	Lacinia's headland unto Juno dear,
3.769	the heights of Caulon, and that sailors' bane,
3.770	ship-shattering Scylaceum. Thence half seen,
3.771	trinacrian Aetna cleaves th' horizon line;
3.772	we hear from far the crash of shouting seas,
3.773	where lifted billows leap the tide-swept sand.
3.774	Father Anchises cried: “'T is none but she—
3.775	Charybdis! Helenus this reef foretold,
3.776	and rocks of dreadful name. O, fly, my men!
3.777	Rise like one man with long, strong sweep of oars!”
3.778	Not unobedient they! First Palinure
3.779	veered to the leftward wave the willing keel,
3.780	and sails and oars together leftward strove.
3.781	We shot to skyward on the arching surge,
3.782	then, as she sank, dropped deeper than the grave;
3.783	thrice bellowed the vast cliffs from vaulted wall;
3.784	thrice saw we spouted foam and showers of stars.
3.785	After these things both wind and sun did fail;
3.786	and weary, worn, not witting of our way,
3.787	we drifted shoreward to the Cyclops' land.
3.788	A spreading bay is there, impregnable
3.789	to all invading storms; and Aetna's throat
3.790	with roar of frightful ruin thunders nigh.
3.791	Now to the realm of light it lifts a cloud
3.792	of pitch-black, whirling smoke, and fiery dust,
3.793	shooting out globes of flame, with monster tongues
3.794	that lick the stars; now huge crags of itself,
3.795	out of the bowels of the mountain torn,
3.796	its maw disgorges, while the molten rock
3.797	rolls screaming skyward; from the nether deep
3.798	the fathomless abyss makes ebb and flow.
3.799	Enceladus, his body lightning-scarred,
3.800	lies prisoned under all, so runs the tale:
3.801	o'er him gigantic Aetna breathes in fire
3.802	from crack and seam; and if he haply turn
3.803	to change his wearied side, Trinacria's isle
3.804	trembles and moans, and thick fumes mantle heaven.
3.805	That night in screen and covert of a grove
3.806	we bore the dire convulsion, unaware
3.807	whence the loud horror came. For not a star
3.808	its lamp allowed, nor burned in upper sky
3.809	the constellated fires, but all was gloom,
3.810	and frowning night confined the moon in cloud.
3.811	When from the eastern waves the light of morn
3.812	began to peer, and from the upper sky
3.813	Aurora flamed away the dark and dew,
3.814	out of the forest sprang a startling shape
3.815	of hunger-wasted misery; a man
3.816	in wretched guise, who shoreward came with hands
3.817	outstretched in supplication. We turned back
3.818	and scanned him well. All grime and foulness he,
3.819	with long and tangled beard, his savage garb
3.820	fastened with thorns; but in all else he seemed
3.821	a Greek, and in his country's league of arms
3.822	sent to the seige of Troy. Then he beheld
3.823	the Dardan habit, and our Trojan steel,
3.824	he somewhat paused, as if in dread dismay
3.825	such sight to see, and falteringly moved;
3.826	but soon with headlong steps he sought the shore,
3.827	ejaculating broken sobs and prayers:
3.828	“By stars above! By gods on high! O, hear!
3.829	By this bright heavenly air we mortals breathe,
3.830	save me, sweet Trojans! Carry me away
3.831	unto what land ye will! I ask no more.
3.832	I came, I know it, in the ships of Greece;
3.833	and I did war, 't is true, with Ilium's gods.
3.834	O, if the crime deserve it, fling my corse
3.835	on yonder waves, and in the boundless brine
3.836	sink me forever! Give me in my death
3.837	the comfort that by human hands I die.”
3.838	He clasped our knees, and writhing on his own
3.839	clung fast. We bid him tell his race and name,
3.840	and by what fate pursued. Anchises gave
3.841	his own right hand in swift and generous aid,
3.842	and by prompt token cheered the exile's heart,
3.843	who, banishing his fears, poured forth this tale :—
3.844	“My home was Ithaca, and I partook
3.845	the fortunes of Ulysses evil-starred.
3.846	My name is Achemenides, my sire
3.847	was Adamastus, and I sailed for Troy,
3.848	being so poor,—O, that I ne'er had change
3.849	the lot I bore! In yon vast Cyclops' cave
3.850	my comrades, flying from its gruesome door,
3.851	left me behind, forgotten. 'T is a house
3.852	of gory feasts of flesh, 't is deep and dark,
3.853	and vaulted high. He looms as high as heaven;
3.854	I pray the blessed gods to rid the earth
3.855	of the vile monster! None can look on him,
3.856	none speak with him. He feeds on clotted gore
3.857	of disembowelled men. These very eyes
3.858	saw him seize two of our own company,
3.859	and, as he lolled back in the cave, he clutched
3.860	and dashed them on the stones, fouling the floor
3.861	with torrent of their blood; myself I saw him
3.862	crunch with his teeth the dripping, bloody limbs
3.863	still hot and pulsing on his hungry jaw.
3.864	But not without reward! For such a sight
3.865	Ulysses would not brook, and Ithaca
3.866	forgot not in such strait the name he bore.
3.867	For soon as, gorged with feasting and o'ercome
3.868	with drunken slumber, the foul giant lay
3.869	sprawled through the cave, his head dropped helpless down,
3.870	disgorging as he slept thick drool of gore
3.871	and gobbets drenched with bloody wine; then we,
3.872	calling on Heaven and taking place by lot,
3.873	drew round him like one man, and with a beam
3.874	sharpened at end bored out that monster eye,
3.875	which, huge and sole, lay under the grim brow,
3.876	round as an Argive shield or Phoebus' star.
3.877	Thus took we joyful vengeance for the shades
3.878	of our lost mates. But, O ill-fated men!
3.879	Fly, I implore, and cut the cables free
3.880	along the beach! For in the land abide,
3.881	like Polyphemus, who in hollow cave
3.882	kept fleecy sheep, and milked his fruitful ewes,
3.883	a hundred other, huge as he, who rove
3.884	wide o'er this winding shore and mountains fair:
3.885	Cyclops accursed, bestial! Thrice the moon
3.886	has filled her horns with light, while here I dwell
3.887	in lonely woods and lairs of creatures wild;
3.888	or from tall cliffs out-peering I discern
3.889	the Cyclops, and shrink shuddering from the sound
3.890	of their vast step and cry. My sorry fare
3.891	is berries and hard corners dropped from trees,
3.892	or herb-roots torn out from the niggard ground.
3.893	Though watching the whole sea, only today
3.894	Have I had sight of ships. To you I fled.
3.895	Whate'er ye be, it was my only prayer
3.896	to 'scape that monster brood. I ask no more.
3.897	O, set me free by any death ye will!”
3.898	He scarce had said, when moving o'er the crest
3.899	of a high hill a giant shape we saw:
3.900	that shepherd Polyphemus, with his flocks
3.901	down-wending to the well-known water-side;
3.902	huge, shapeless, horrible, with blinded eye,
3.903	bearing a lopped pine for a staff, he made
3.904	his footing sure, while the white, fleecy sheep,
3.905	sole pleasure now, and solace of his woes,
3.906	ran huddling at his side.
3.907	Soon to the vast flood of the level brine
3.908	he came, and washed the flowing gore away
3.909	from that out-hollowed eye; he gnashed his teeth,
3.910	groaning, and deep into the watery way
3.911	stalked on, his tall bulk wet by scarce a wave.
3.912	We fled in haste, though far, and with us bore
3.913	the truthful suppliant; cut silently
3.914	the anchor-ropes, and, bending to the oar,
3.915	swept on with eager strokes clean out to sea.
3.916	Aware he was, and toward our loud halloo
3.917	whirled sudden round; but when no power had he
3.918	to seize or harm, nor could his fierce pursuit
3.919	o'ertake the Ionian surges as they rolled,
3.920	he raised a cry incredible; the sea
3.921	with all its billows trembled; the wide shore
3.922	of Italy from glens and gorges moaned,
3.923	and Aetna roared from every vaulted cave.
3.924	Then rallied from the grove-clad, Iofty isle
3.925	the Cyclops' clan, and lined the beach and bay.
3.926	We saw each lonely eyeball glare in vain,
3.927	as side by side those brothers Aetna-born
3.928	stood towering high, a conclave dark and dire:
3.929	as when, far up some mountain's famous crest,
3.930	wind-fronting oaks or cone-clad cypresses
3.931	have made assembling in the solemn hills,
3.932	Jove's giant wood or Dian's sacred grove.
3.933	We, terror-struck, would fly we knew not where,
3.934	with loosened sheet and canvas swelling strong
3.935	before a welcome wind; but Helenus
3.936	bade us both Scylla and Charybdis fear,
3.937	where 'twixt the twain death straitly hems the way;
3.938	and so the counsel was to veer our bark
3.939	the course it came. But lo! a northern gale
3.940	burst o'er us from Pelorus' narrowed side,
3.941	and on we rode far past Pantagia's bay
3.942	of unhewn rock, and past the haven strong
3.943	of Megara, and Thapsus Iying low.
3.944	Such were the names retold, and such the shores
3.945	shown us by Achemenides, whose fate
3.946	made him familiar there, for he had sailed
3.947	with evil-starred Ulysses o'er that sea.
3.948	Off the Sicilian shore an island lies,
3.949	wave-washed Plemmyrium, called in olden days
3.950	Ortygia; here Alpheus, river-god,
3.951	from Elis flowed by secret sluice, they say,
3.952	beneath the sea, and mingles at thy mouth,
3.953	fair Arethusa! with Sicilian waves.
3.954	Our voices hailed the great gods of the land
3.955	with reverent prayer; then skirted we the shore,
3.956	where smooth Helorus floods the fruitful plain.
3.957	Under Pachynus' beetling precipice
3.958	we kept our course; then Camarina rose
3.959	in distant view, firm-seated evermore
3.960	by Fate's decree; and that far-spreading vale
3.961	of Gela, with the name of power it takes
3.962	from its wide river; and, uptowering far,
3.963	the ramparts of proud Acragas appeared,
3.964	where fiery steeds were bred in days of old.
3.965	Borne by the winds, along thy coast I fled,
3.966	Selinus, green with palm! and past the shore
3.967	of Lilybaeum with its treacherous reef;
3.968	till at the last the port of Drepanum
3.969	received me to its melancholy strand.
3.970	Here, woe is me I outworn by stormful seas,
3.971	my sire, sole comfort of my grievous doom,
3.972	Anchises ceased to be. O best of sires!
3.973	Here didst thou leave me in the weary way;
3.974	through all our perils—O the bitter loss! —
3.975	borne safely, but in vain. King Helenus,
3.976	whose prophet-tongue of dark events foretold,
3.977	spoke not this woe; nor did Celeno's curse
3.978	of this forebode. Such my last loss and pain;
3.979	such, of my weary way, the destined goal.
3.980	From thence departing, the divine behest
3.981	impelled me to thy shores, O listening queen!
3.982	Such was, while all gave ear, the tale sublime
3.983	father Aeneas, none but he, set forth
3.984	of wanderings and of dark decrees divine:
3.985	silent at last, he ceased, and took repose.
4.1	Now felt the Queen the sharp, slow-gathering pangs
4.2	of love; and out of every pulsing vein
4.3	nourished the wound and fed its viewless fire.
4.4	Her hero's virtues and his lordly line
4.5	keep calling to her soul; his words, his glance,
4.6	cling to her heart like lingering, barbed steel,
4.7	and rest and peace from her vexed body fly.
4.8	A new day's dawn with Phoebus' lamp divine
4.9	lit up all lands, and from the vaulted heaven
4.10	Aurora had dispelled the dark and dew;
4.11	when thus unto the ever-answering heart
4.12	of her dear sister spoke the stricken Queen:
4.13	“Anna, my sister, what disturbing dreams
4.14	perplex me and alarm? What guest is this
4.15	new-welcomed to our house? How proud his mien!
4.16	What dauntless courage and exploits of war!
4.17	Sooth, I receive it for no idle tale
4.18	that of the gods he sprang. 'T is cowardice
4.19	betrays the base-born soul. Ah me! How fate
4.20	has smitten him with storms! What dire extremes
4.21	of war and horror in his tale he told!
4.22	O, were it not immutably resolved
4.23	in my fixed heart, that to no shape of man
4.24	I would be wed again (since my first love
4.25	left me by death abandoned and betrayed);
4.26	loathed I not so the marriage torch and train,
4.27	I could—who knows?—to this one weakness yield.
4.28	Anna, I hide it not! But since the doom
4.29	of my ill-starred Sichaeus, when our shrines
4.30	were by a brother's murder dabbled o'er,
4.31	this man alone has moved me; he alone
4.32	has shaken my weak will. I seem to feel
4.33	the motions of love's lost, familiar fire.
4.34	But may the earth gape open where I tread,
4.35	and may almighty Jove with thunder-scourge
4.36	hurl me to Erebus' abysmal shade,
4.37	to pallid ghosts and midnight fathomless,
4.38	before, O Chastity! I shall offend
4.39	thy holy power, or cast thy bonds away!
4.40	He who first mingled his dear life with mine
4.41	took with him all my heart. 'T is his alone —
4.42	o, let it rest beside him in the grave!”
4.43	She spoke: the bursting tears her breast o'erflowed.
4.44	“O dearer to thy sister than her life,”
4.45	Anna replied, “wouldst thou in sorrow's weed
4.46	waste thy long youth alone, nor ever know
4.47	sweet babes at thine own breast, nor gifts of love?
4.48	Will dust and ashes, or a buried ghost
4.49	reck what we do? 'T is true thy grieving heart
4.50	was cold to earlier wooers, Libya's now,
4.51	and long ago in Tyre. Iarbas knew
4.52	thy scorn, and many a prince and captain bred
4.53	in Afric's land of glory. Why resist
4.54	a love that makes thee glad? Hast thou no care
4.55	what alien lands are these where thou dost reign?
4.56	Here are Gaetulia's cities and her tribes
4.57	unconquered ever; on thy borders rove
4.58	Numidia's uncurbed cavalry; here too
4.59	lies Syrtis' cruel shore, and regions wide
4.60	of thirsty desert, menaced everywhere
4.61	by the wild hordes of Barca. Shall I tell
4.62	of Tyre's hostilities, the threats and rage
4.63	of our own brother? Friendly gods, I bow,
4.64	wafted the Teucrian ships, with Juno's aid,
4.65	to these our shores. O sister, what a throne,
4.66	and what imperial city shall be thine,
4.67	if thus espoused! With Trojan arms allied
4.68	how far may not our Punic fame extend
4.69	in deeds of power? Call therefore on the gods
4.70	to favor thee; and, after omens fair,
4.71	give queenly welcome, and contrive excuse
4.72	to make him tarry, while yon wintry seas
4.73	are loud beneath Orion's stormful star,
4.74	and on his battered ships the season frowns.”
4.75	So saying, she stirred a passion-burning breast
4.76	to Iove more madly still; her words infused
4.77	a doubting mind with hope, and bade the blush
4.78	of shame begone. First to the shrines they went
4.79	and sued for grace; performing sacrifice,
4.80	choosing an offering of unblemished ewes,
4.81	to law-bestowing Ceres, to the god
4.82	of light, to sire Lyeus, Iord of wine;
4.83	but chiefly unto Juno, patroness
4.84	of nuptial vows. There Dido, beauteous Queen
4.85	held forth in her right hand the sacred bowl
4.86	and poured it full between the lifted horns
4.87	of the white heifer; or on temple floors
4.88	she strode among the richly laden shrines,
4.89	the eyes of gods upon her, worshipping
4.90	with many a votive gift; or, peering deep
4.91	into the victims' cloven sides, she read
4.92	the fate-revealing tokens trembling there.
4.93	How blind the hearts of prophets be! Alas!
4.94	Of what avail be temples and fond prayers
4.95	to change a frenzied mind? Devouring ever,
4.96	love's fire burns inward to her bones; she feels
4.97	quick in her breast the viewless, voiceless wound.
4.98	Ill-fated Dido ranges up and down
4.99	the spaces of her city, desperate
4.100	her life one flame—like arrow-stricken doe
4.101	through Cretan forest rashly wandering,
4.102	pierced by a far-off shepherd, who pursues
4.103	with shafts, and leaves behind his light-winged steed,
4.104	not knowing; while she scours the dark ravines
4.105	of Dicte and its woodlands; at her heart
4.106	the mortal barb irrevocably clings.
4.107	around her city's battlements she guides
4.108	aeneas, to make show of Sidon's gold,
4.109	and what her realm can boast; full oft her voice
4.110	essays to speak and frembling dies away:
4.111	or, when the daylight fades, she spreads anew
4.112	a royal banquet, and once more will plead
4.113	mad that she is, to hear the Trojan sorrow;
4.114	and with oblivious ravishment once more
4.115	hangs on his lips who tells; or when her guests
4.116	are scattered, and the wan moon's fading horn
4.117	bedims its ray, while many a sinking star
4.118	invites to slumber, there she weeps alone
4.119	in the deserted hall, and casts her down
4.120	on the cold couch he pressed. Her love from far
4.121	beholds her vanished hero and receives
4.122	his voice upon her ears; or to her breast,
4.123	moved by a father's image in his child,
4.124	she clasps Ascanius, seeking to deceive
4.125	her unblest passion so. Her enterprise
4.126	of tower and rampart stops: her martial host
4.127	no Ionger she reviews, nor fashions now
4.128	defensive haven and defiant wall;
4.129	but idly all her half-built bastions frown,
4.130	and enginery of sieges, high as heaven.
4.131	But soon the chosen spouse of Jove perceived
4.132	the Queen's infection; and because the voice
