8.652	and fired his regal dwellings; he, the while,
8.653	escaped immediate death and fied away
8.654	to the Rutulian land, to find defence
8.655	in Turnus hospitality. To-day
8.656	Etruria, to righteous anger stirred,
8.657	demands with urgent arms her guilty King.
8.658	To their large host, Aeneas, I will give
8.659	an added strength, thyself. For yonder shores
8.660	re-echo with the tumult and the cry
8.661	of ships in close array; their eager lords
8.662	are clamoring for battle. But the song
8.663	of the gray omen-giver thus declares
8.664	their destiny: ‘O goodly princes born
8.665	of old Maeonian lineage! Ye that are
8.666	the bloom and glory of an ancient race,
8.667	whom just occasions now and noble rage
8.668	enflame against Mezentius your foe,
8.669	it is decreed that yonder nation proud
8.670	shall never submit to chiefs Italian-born.
8.671	Seek ye a king from far!’ So in the field
8.672	inert and fearful lies Etruria's force,
8.673	disarmed by oracles. Their Tarchon sent
8.674	envoys who bore a sceptre and a crown
8.675	even to me, and prayed I should assume
8.676	the sacred emblems of Etruria's king,
8.677	and lead their host to war. But unto me
8.678	cold, sluggish age, now barren and outworn,
8.679	denies new kingdoms, and my slow-paced powers
8.680	run to brave deeds no more. Nor could I urge
8.681	my son, who by his Sabine mother's line
8.682	is half Italian-born. Thyself art he,
8.683	whose birth illustrious and manly prime
8.684	fate favors and celestial powers approve.
8.685	Therefore go forth, O bravest chief and King
8.686	of Troy and Italy! To thee I give
8.687	the hope and consolation of our throne,
8.688	pallas, my son, and bid him find in thee
8.689	a master and example, while he learns
8.690	the soldier's arduous toil. With thy brave deeds
8.691	let him familiar grow, and reverence thee
8.692	with youthful love and honor. In his train
8.693	two hundred horsemen of Arcadia,
8.694	our choicest men-at-arms, shall ride; and he
8.695	in his own name an equal band shall bring
8.696	to follow only thee.” Such the discourse.
8.697	With meditative brows and downcast eyes
8.698	Aeneas and Achates, sad at heart,
8.699	mused on unnumbered perils yet to come.
8.700	But out of cloudless sky Cythera's Queen
8.701	gave sudden signal: from th' ethereal dome
8.702	a thunder-peal and flash of quivering fire
8.703	tumultuous broke, as if the world would fall,
8.704	and bellowing Tuscan trumpets shook the air.
8.705	All eyes look up. Again and yet again
8.706	crashed the terrible din, and where the sky
8.707	looked clearest hung a visionary cloud,
8.708	whence through the brightness blazed resounding arms.
8.709	All hearts stood still. But Troy's heroic son
8.710	knew that his mother in the skies redeemed
8.711	her pledge in sound of thunder: so he cried,
8.712	“Seek not, my friend, seek not thyself to read
8.713	the meaning of the omen. 'T is to me
8.714	Olympus calls. My goddess-mother gave
8.715	long since her promise of a heavenly sign
8.716	if war should burst; and that her power would bring
8.717	a panoply from Vulcan through the air,
8.718	to help us at our need. Alas, what deaths
8.719	over Laurentum's ill-starred host impend!
8.720	O Turnus, what a reckoning thou shalt pay
8.721	to me in arms! O Tiber, in thy wave
8.722	what helms and shields and mighty soldiers slain
8.723	shall in confusion roll! Yea, let them lead
8.724	their lines to battle, and our league abjure!”
8.725	He said: and from the lofty throne uprose.
8.726	Straightway he roused anew the slumbering fire
8.727	sacred to Hercules, and glad at heart
8.728	adored, as yesterday, the household gods
8.729	revered by good Evander, at whose side
8.730	the Trojan company made sacrifice
8.731	of chosen lambs, with fitting rites and true.
8.732	Then to his ships he tried him, and rejoined
8.733	his trusty followers, of whom he took
8.734	the best for valor known, to lend him aid
8.735	in deeds of war. Others he bade return
8.736	down stream in easy course, and tidings bear
8.737	to young Ascanius of the new event,
8.738	and of his father. Horses then were brought
8.739	for all the Teucrians to Etruria bound;
8.740	and for Aeneas one of rarest breed,
8.741	o'er whom a tawny robe descended low,
8.742	of lion-skin, with claws of gleaming gold.
8.743	Noised swiftly through the little town it flies
8.744	that to the precinct of the Tuscan King
8.745	armed horsemen speed. Pale mothers in great fear
8.746	unceasing pray; for panic closely runs
8.747	in danger's steps; the war-god drawing nigh
8.748	looms larger; and good sire Evander now
8.749	clings to the hand of his departing son
8.750	and, weeping without stay, makes sad farewell:
8.751	“O, that great Jove would give me once again
8.752	my vanished years! O, if such man I were,
8.753	as when beneath Praeneste's wall I slew
8.754	the front ranks of her sons, and burned for spoil
8.755	their gathered shields on my triumph day;
8.756	or when this right hand hurled king Erulus
8.757	to shades below, though—terrible to tell —
8.758	Feronia bore him with three lives, that thrice
8.759	he might arise from deadly strife o'erthrown,
8.760	and thrice be slain—yet all these lives took I,
8.761	and of his arms despoiled him o'er and o'er:
8.762	not now, sweet son (if such lost might were mine),
8.763	should I from thy beloved embrace be torn;
8.764	nor could Mezentius with insulting sword
8.765	do murder in my sight and make my land
8.766	depopulate and forlorn. O gods in Heaven,
8.767	and chiefly thou whom all the gods obey,
8.768	have pity, Jove, upon Arcadia's King,
8.769	and hear a father's prayer: if your intent
8.770	be for my Pallas a defence secure,
8.771	if it be writ that long as I shall live,
8.772	my eyes may see him, and my arms enfold,
8.773	I pray for life, and all its ills I bear.
8.774	But if some curse, too dark to tell, impend
8.775	from thee, O Fortune blind! I pray thee break
8.776	my thread of miserable life to-day;
8.777	to-day, while fear still doubts and hope still smiles
8.778	on the unknown to-morrow, as I hold
8.779	thee to my bosom, dearest child, who art
8.780	my last and only joy; to-day, before
8.781	th' intolerable tidings smite my ears.”
8.782	Such grief the royal father's heart outpoured
8.783	at this last parting; the strong arms of slaves
8.784	lifted him, fallen in swoon, and bore him home.
8.785	Now forth beneath the wide-swung city-gates
8.786	the mounted squadron poured; Aeneas rode,
8.787	companioned of Achates, in the van;
8.788	then other lords of Troy. There Pallas shone
8.789	conspicuous in the midmost line, with cloak
8.790	and blazoned arms, as when the Morning-star
8.791	(To Venus dearest of all orbs that burn),
8.792	out of his lucent bath in ocean wave
8.793	lifts to the skies his countenance divine,
8.794	and melts the shadows of the night away.
8.795	Upon the ramparts trembling matrons stand
8.796	and follow with dimmed eyes the dusty cloud
8.797	whence gleam the brazen arms. The warriors ride
8.798	straight on through brake and fell, the nearest way;
8.799	loud ring the war-cries, and in martial line
8.800	the pounding hoof-beats shake the crumbling ground.
8.801	By Caere's cold flood lies an ample grove
8.802	revered from age to age. The hollowing hills
8.803	enclasp it in wide circles of dark fir,
8.804	and the Pelasgians, so the legends tell,
8.805	primaeval settlers of the Latin plains,
8.806	called it the haunt of Silvan, kindly god
8.807	of flocks and fields, and honoring the grove
8.808	gave it a festal day. Hard by this spot
8.809	had Tarchon with the Tuscans fortified
8.810	his bivouac, and from the heights afar
8.811	his legions could be seen in wide array
8.812	outstretching through the plain. To meet them there
8.813	Aeneas and his veteran chivalry
8.814	made sure advance, and found repose at eve
8.815	for warrior travel-worn and fainting steed.
8.816	But now athwart the darkening air of heaven
8.817	came Venus gleaming bright, to bring her son
8.818	the gifts divine. In deep, sequestered vale
8.819	she found him by a cooling rill retired,
8.820	and hailed him thus: “Behold the promised gift,
8.821	by craft and power of my Olympian spouse
8.822	made perfect, that my son need never fear
8.823	Laurentum's haughty host, nor to provoke
8.824	fierce Turnus to the fray.” Cythera's Queen
8.825	so saying, embraced her son, and hung the arms,
8.826	all glittering, on an oak that stood thereby.
8.827	The hero, with exultant heart and proud,
8.828	gazing unwearied at his mother's gift,
8.829	surveys them close, and poises in his hands
8.830	the helmet's dreadful crest and glancing flame,
8.831	the sword death-dealing, and the corselet strong,
8.832	impenetrable brass, blood-red and large,
8.833	like some dark-lowering, purple cloud that gleams
8.834	beneath the smiting sun and flashes far
8.835	its answering ray; and burnished greaves were there,
8.836	fine gold and amber; then the spear and shield —
8.837	the shield—of which the blazonry divine
8.838	exceeds all power to tell. Thereon were seen
8.839	Italia's story and triumphant Rome,
8.840	wrought by the Lord of Fire, who was not blind
8.841	to lore inspired and prophesying song,
8.842	fore-reading things to come. He pictured there
8.843	Iulus' destined line of glorious sons
8.844	marshalled for many a war. In cavern green,
8.845	haunt of the war-god, lay the mother-wolf;
8.846	the twin boy-sucklings at her udders played,
8.847	nor feared such nurse; with long neck backward thrown
8.848	she fondled each, and shaped with busy tongue
8.849	their bodies fair. Near these were pictured well
8.850	the walls of Rome and ravished Sabine wives
8.851	in the thronged theatre violently seized,
8.852	when the great games were done; then, sudden war
8.853	of Romulus against the Cures grim
8.854	and hoary Tatius; next, the end of strife
8.855	between the rival kings, who stood in arms
8.856	before Jove's sacred altar, cup in hand,
8.857	and swore a compact o'er the slaughtered swine.
8.858	Hard by, behold, the whirling chariots tore
8.859	Mettus asunder (would thou hadst been true,
8.860	false Alban, to thy vow!); and Tullus trailed
8.861	the traitor's mangled corse along the hills,
8.862	the wild thorn dripping gore. Porsenna, next,
8.863	sent to revolted Rome his proud command
8.864	to take her Tarquin back, and with strong siege
8.865	assailed the city's wall; while unsubdued
8.866	Aeneas' sons took arms in freedom's name.
8.867	there too the semblance of the frustrate King,
8.868	a semblance of his wrath and menace vain,
8.869	when Cocles broke the bridge, and Cloelia burst
8.870	her captive bonds and swam the Tiber's wave.
8.871	Lo, on the steep Tarpeian citadel
8.872	stood Manlius at the sacred doors of Jove,
8.873	holding the capitol, whereon was seen
8.874	the fresh-thatched house of Romulus the King.
8.875	There, too, all silver, through arcade of gold
8.876	fluttered the goose, whose monitory call
8.877	revealed the foeman at the gate: outside
8.878	besieging Gauls the thorny pathway climbed,
8.879	ambushed in shadow and the friendly dark
8.880	of night without a star; their flowing hair
8.881	was golden, and their every vesture gold;
8.882	their cloaks were glittering plaid; each milk-white neck
8.883	bore circlet of bright gold; in each man's hand
8.884	two Alpine javelins gleamed, and for defence
8.885	long shields the wild northern warriors bore.
8.886	There, graven cunningly, the Salian choir
8.887	went leaping, and in Lupercalian feast
8.888	the naked striplings ran; while others, crowned
8.889	with peaked cap, bore shields that fell from heaven;
8.890	and, bearing into Rome their emblems old,
8.891	chaste priestesses on soft-strewn litters passed.
8.892	But far from these th' artificer divine
8.893	had wrought a Tartarus, the dreadful doors
8.894	of Pluto, and the chastisements of sin;
8.895	swung o'er a threatening precipice, was seen
8.896	thy trembling form, O Catiline, in fear
8.897	of fury-faces nigh: and distant far
8.898	th' assemblies of the righteous, in whose midst
8.899	was Cato, giving judgment and decree.
8.900	Encircled by these pictures ran the waves
8.901	of vast, unrestful seas in flowing gold,
8.902	where seemed along the azure crests to fly
8.903	the hoary foam, and in a silver ring
8.904	the tails of swift, emerging dolphins lashed
8.905	the waters bright, and clove the tumbling brine.
8.906	For the shield's central glory could be seen
8.907	great fleets of brazen galleys, and the fight
8.908	at Actium; where, ablaze with war's array,
8.909	Leucate's peak glowed o'er the golden tide.
8.910	Caesar Augustus led Italia's sons
8.911	to battle: at his side concordant moved
8.912	Senate and Roman People, with their gods
8.913	of hearth and home, and all Olympian Powers.
8.914	Uplifted on his ship he stands; his brows
8.915	beneath a double glory smile, and bright
8.916	over his forehead beams the Julian star.
8.917	in neighboring region great Agrippa leads,
8.918	by favor of fair winds and friendly Heaven,
8.919	his squadron forth: upon his brows he wears
8.920	the peerless emblem of his rostral crown.
8.921	Opposing, in barbaric splendor shine
8.922	the arms of Antony: in victor's garb
8.923	from nations in the land of morn he rides,
8.924	and from the Red Sea, bringing in his train
8.925	Egypt and Syria, utmost Bactria's horde,
8.926	and last—O shameless!—his Egyptian spouse.
8.927	All to the fight make haste; the slanted oars
8.928	and triple beaks of brass uptear the waves
8.929	to angry foam, as to the deep they speed
8.930	like hills on hill-tops hurled, or Cyclades
8.931	drifting and clashing in the sea: so vast
8.932	that shock of castled ships and mighty men!
8.933	Swift, arrowy steel and balls of blazing tow
8.934	rain o'er the waters, till the sea-god's world
8.935	flows red with slaughter. In the midst, the Queen,
8.936	sounding her native timbrel, wildly calls
8.937	her minions to the fight, nor yet can see
8.938	two fatal asps behind. Her monster-gods,
8.939	barking Anubis, and his mongrel crew,
8.940	on Neptune, Venus, and Minerva fling
8.941	their impious arms; the face of angry Mars,
8.942	carved out of iron, in the centre frowns,
8.943	grim Furies fill the air; Discordia strides
8.944	in rent robe, mad with joy; and at her side,
8.945	bellona waves her sanguinary scourge.
8.946	There Actian Apollo watched the war,
8.947	and o'er it stretched his bow; which when they knew,
8.948	Egyptian, Arab, and swart Indian slave,
8.949	and all the sons of Saba fled away
8.950	in terror of his arm. The vanquished Queen
8.951	made prayer to all the winds, and more and more
8.952	flung out the swelling sail: on wind-swept wave
8.953	she fled through dead and dying; her white brow
8.954	the Lord of Fire had cunningly portrayed
8.955	blanched with approaching doom. Beyond her lay
8.956	the large-limbed picture of the mournful Nile,
8.957	who from his bosom spread his garments wide,
8.958	and offered refuge in his sheltering streams
8.959	and broad, blue breast, to all her fallen power.
8.960	But Caesar in his triple triumph passed
8.961	the gates of Rome, and gave Italia's gods,
8.962	for grateful offering and immortal praise,
8.963	three hundred temples; all the city streets
8.964	with game and revel and applauding song
8.965	rang loud; in all the temples altars burned
8.966	and Roman matrons prayed; the slaughtered herds
8.967	strewed well the sacred ground. The hero, throned
8.968	at snow-white marble threshold of the fane
8.969	to radiant Phoebus, views the gift and spoil
8.970	the nations bring, and on the portals proud
8.971	hangs a perpetual garland: in long file
8.972	the vanquished peoples pass, of alien tongues,
8.973	of arms and vesture strange. Here Vulcan showed
8.974	ungirdled Afric chiefs and Nomads bold,
8.975	Gelonian bowmen, men of Caria,
8.976	and Leleges. Euphrates seemed to flow
8.977	with humbler wave; the world's remotest men,
8.978	Morini came, with double-horned Rhine,
8.979	and Dahae, little wont to bend the knee,
8.980	and swift Araxes, for a bridge too proud.
8.981	Such was the blazoned shield his mother gave
8.982	from Vulcan's forge; which with astonished eyes
8.983	Aeneas viewed, and scanned with joyful mind
8.984	such shadows of an unknown age to be;
8.985	then on his shoulder for a burden bore
8.986	the destined mighty deeds of all his sons.
9.1	While thus in distant region moves the war,
9.2	down to bold Turnus Saturn's daughter sends
9.3	celestial Iris. In a sacred vale,
9.4	the seat of worship at his grandsire's tomb,
9.5	Pilumnus, Faunus' son, the hero mused.
9.6	And thus the wonder-child of Thaumas called
9.7	with lips of rose: “O Turnus, what no god
9.8	dared give for reward of thy fondest vow,
9.9	has come unbidden on its destined day.
9.10	Behold, Aeneas, who has left behind
9.11	the city with his fleet and followers,
9.12	is gone to kingly Palatine, the home
9.13	of good Evander. Yea, his march invades
9.14	the far Etrurian towns, where now he arms
9.15	the Lydian rustics. Wilt thou longer muse?
9.16	Call for thy chariot and steeds! Away!
9.17	Take yonder tents by terror and surprise!”
9.18	She spoke; and heavenward on poising wings
9.19	soared, cleaving as she fled from cloud to cloud
9.20	a vast, resplendent bow. The warrior saw,
9.21	and, lifting both his hands, pursued with prayer
9.22	the fading glory: “Beauteous Iris, hail!
9.23	Proud ornament of heaven! who sent thee here
9.24	across yon cloud to earth, and unto me?
9.25	Whence may this sudden brightness fall? I see
9.26	the middle welkin lift, and many a star,
9.27	far-wandering in the sky. Such solemn sign
9.28	I shall obey, and thee, O god unknown!”
9.29	So saying, he turned him to a sacred stream,
9.30	took water from its brim, and offered Heaven
9.31	much prayer, with many an importuning vow.
9.32	Soon o'er the spreading fields in proud array
9.33	the gathered legions poured; no lack was there
9.34	of steeds all fire, and broidered pomp and gold.
9.35	Messapus led the van; in rearguard rode
9.36	the sons of Tyrrheus; kingly Turnus towered
9.37	from the mid-column eminent: the host
9.38	moved as great Ganges lifting silently
9.39	his seven peaceful streams, or when the flood
9.40	of fructifying Nile from many a field
9.41	back to his channel flows. A swift-blown cloud
9.42	of black, uprolling dust the Teucrians see
9.43	o'ershadowing the plain; Calcus calls
9.44	from lofty outpost: “O my countrymen,
9.45	I see a huge, black ball of rolling smoke.
9.46	Your swords and lances! Man the walls! To arms!
9.47	The foe is here! What ho!” With clamors loud
9.48	the Teucrians through the city-gates retire,
9.49	and muster on the walls. For, wise in war,
9.50	Aeneas, ere he went, had left command
9.51	they should not range in battle-line, nor dare,
9.52	whate'er might hap, to risk in open plain
9.53	the bold sortie, but keep them safe entrenched
9.54	in mounded walls. So now, though rage and shame
9.55	prick to a close fight, they defensive bar
9.56	each portal strong, and, patient of control,
9.57	from hollow towers expect th' encircling foe.
9.58	Turnus, at full speed, had outridden far
9.59	his laggard host, and, leading in his train
9.60	a score of chosen knights, dashed into view
9.61	hard by the walls. A barb of Thracian breed
9.62	dappled with white he rode; a crimson plume
9.63	flamed over his golden helmet. “Who,” he cries,
9.64	“Is foremost at the foe? Who follows me?
9.65	Behold!” And, with the word, he hurled in air
9.66	a javelin, provoking instant war:
9.67	and, towering from his horse, charged o'er the field.
9.68	With answering shout his men-at-arms pursue,
9.69	and war-cries terrible. They laugh to scorn
9.70	“the craven hearts of Troy, that cannot give
9.71	fair, equal vantage, matching man to man,
9.72	but cuddle into camp.” This way and that
9.73	Turnus careers, and stormily surveys
9.74	the frowning rampart, and where way is none
9.75	some entering breach would find: so prowls a wolf
9.76	nigh the full sheepfold, and through wind and rain
9.77	stands howling at the postern all night long;
9.78	beneath the ewes their bleating lambs lie safe;
9.79	but he, with undesisting fury, more
9.80	rages from far, made frantic for his prey
9.81	by hunger of long hours, his foaming jaws
9.82	athirst for blood: not less the envy burned
9.83	of the Rutulian, as he scanned in vain
9.84	the stronghold of his foe. Indignant scorn
9.85	thrilled all his iron frame. But how contrive
9.86	to storm the fortress or by force expel
9.87	the Trojans from the rampart, and disperse
9.88	along the plain? Straightway he spied the ships,
9.89	in hiding near the camp, defended well
9.90	by mounded river-bank and fleeting wave.
9.91	On these he fell; while his exultant crew
9.92	brought firebrands, and he with heart aflame
9.93	grasped with a vengeful hand the blazing pine.
9.94	To the wild work his followers sped; for who
9.95	could prove him craven under Turnus' eye?
9.96	The whole troop for the weapon of their rage
9.97	seized smoking coals, of many a hearth the spoil;
9.98	red glare of fuming torches burned abroad,
9.99	and Vulcan starward flung a sparkling cloud.
9.100	What god, O Muses, saved the Trojans then
9.101	from wrathful flame? Who shielded then the fleet,
9.102	I pray you tell, from bursting storm of fire?
9.103	From hoary eld the tale, but its renown
9.104	sings on forever. When Aeneas first
9.105	on Phrygian Ida hewed the sacred wood
9.106	for rib and spar, and soon would put to sea,
9.107	that mighty mother of the gods, they say,
9.108	the Berecynthian goddess, thus to Jove
9.109	addressed her plea: “Grant, O my son, a boon,
9.110	which thy dear mother asks, who aided thee
9.111	to quell Olympian war. A grove I have
9.112	of sacred pine, long-loved from year to year.
9.113	On lofty hill it grew, and thither came
9.114	my worshippers with gifts, in secret gloom
9.115	of pine-trees dark and shadowing maple-boughs.;
9.116	these on the Dardan warrior at his need
9.117	I, not unwilling, for his fleet bestowed.
9.118	But I have fears. O, Iet a parent's prayer
9.119	in this prevail, and bid my care begone!
9.120	Let not rude voyages nor the shock of storm
9.121	my ships subdue, but let their sacred birth
9.122	on my charmed hills their strength and safety be!”
9.123	Then spake her son, who guides the wheeling spheres:
9.124	“Wouldst thou, my mother, strive to oversway
9.125	the course of Fate? What means this prayer of thine?
9.126	Can it be granted ships of mortal mould
9.127	to wear immortal being? Wouldst thou see
9.128	Aeneas pass undoubting and secure
9.129	through doubtful strait and peril? On what god
9.130	was e'er such power bestowed? Yet will I grant
9.131	a different boon. Whatever ships shall find
9.132	a safe Ausonian haven, and convey
9.133	safe through the seas to yon Laurentian plain
9.134	the Dardan King, from such I will remove
9.135	their perishable shapes, and bid them be
9.136	sea-nymphs divine, like Nereus' daughters fair,
9.137	Doto and Galatea, whose white breasts
9.138	divide the foaming wave.” He said, and swore
9.139	by his Tartarean brother's mournful stream,
9.140	the pitch-black floods and dark engulfing shore
9.141	of Styx; then great Jove bowed his head, and all
9.142	Olympus quaked at his consenting brow.
9.143	Now was the promised day at hand (for Fate
9.144	had woven the web so far) when Turnus' rage
9.145	stirred the divine progenitress to save
9.146	her sacred ships from fire. Then sudden shone
9.147	a strange effulgence in the eastern air;
9.148	and in a storm-cloud wafted o'er the sky
9.149	were Corybantic choirs, whose dreadful song
9.150	smote both on Teucrian and Rutulian ear:
9.151	“O Teucrians, fear not for the sure defence
9.152	of all the ships, nor arm your mortal hands.
9.153	Yon impious Turnus shall burn up the seas
9.154	before my pine-trees blest. Arise! Be free,
9.155	ye goddesses of ocean, and obey
9.156	your mother's mighty word.” Then instant broke
9.157	the hawsers of the sterns; the beaked prows
9.158	went plunging like great dolphins from the shore
9.159	down to the deeps, and, wonderful to tell,
9.160	the forms of virgin goddesses uprose,
9.161	one for each ship, and seaward sped away.
9.162	The hearts of the Rutulian host stood still
9.163	in panic, and Messapus terrified
9.164	his trembling horses reined; the sacred stream
9.165	of Father Tiber, harshly murmuring,
9.166	held back his flood and checked his seaward way.
9.167	But Turnus' courage failed not; he alone
9.168	his followers roused, and with reproachful words
9.169	alone spoke forth: “These signs and prodigies
9.170	threaten the Trojan only. Jove himself
9.171	has stripped them of their wonted strength: no more
9.172	can they abide our deadly sword and fire.
9.173	The Trojan path to sea is shut. What hope
9.174	of flight is left them now? The half their cause
9.175	is fallen. The possession of this land
9.176	is ours already; thousands of sharp swords
9.177	Italia's nations bring. Small fear have I
9.178	of Phrygia's boasted omens. What to me
9.179	their oracles from heaven? The will of Fate
9.180	and Venus have achieved their uttermost
9.181	in casting on Ausonia's fruitful shore
9.182	yon sons of Troy. I too have destinies:
9.183	and mine, good match for theirs, with this true blade
9.184	will spill the blood of all the baneful brood,
9.185	in vengeance for my stolen wife. Such wrongs
9.186	move not on Atreus' sons alone, nor rouse
9.187	only Mycenae to a righteous war.
9.188	Say you, ‘Troy falls but once?’ One crime, say I,
9.189	should have contented them; and now their souls
9.190	should little less than loathe all womankind.
9.191	These are the sort of soldiers that be brave
9.192	behind entrenchment, where the moated walls
9.193	may stem the foe and make a little room
9.194	betwixt themselves and death. Did they not see
9.195	how Troy's vast bulwark built by Neptune's hand
9.196	crumbled in flame? Forward, my chosen brave!
9.197	Who follows me to cleave his deadly way
9.198	through yonder battlement, and leap like storm
9.199	upon its craven guard? I have no need
9.200	of arms from Vulcan's smithy; nor of ships
9.201	a thousand strong against our Teucrian foes,
9.202	though all Etruria's league enlarge their power.
9.203	Let them not fear dark nights, nor coward theft
9.204	of Pallas' shrine, nor murdered sentinels
9.205	on their acropolis. We shall not hide
9.206	in blinding belly of a horse. But I
9.207	in public eye and open day intend
9.208	to compass their weak wall with siege and fire.
9.209	I'll prove them we be no Pelasgic band,
9.210	no Danaan warriors, such as Hector's arm
9.211	ten years withstood. But look! this day hath spent
9.212	its better part. In what remains, rejoice
9.213	in noble deeds well done; let weary flesh
9.214	have rest and food. My warriors, husband well
9.215	your strength against to-morrow's hopeful war.”
9.216	Meanwhile to block their gates with wakeful guard
9.217	is made Messapus' work, and to gird round
9.218	their camp with watchfires. Then a chosen band,
9.219	twice seven Rutulian chieftains, man the walls
9.220	with soldiery; each leads a hundred men
9.221	crested with crimson, armed with glittering gold.
9.222	Some post to separate sentries, and prepare
9.223	alternate vigil; others, couched on grass,
9.224	laugh round the wine and lift the brazen bowls.
9.225	The camp-fires cheerly burn; the jovial guard
9.226	spend the long, sleepless night in sport and game.
9.227	The Trojans peering from the lofty walls
9.228	survey the foe, and arm for sure defence
9.229	of every point exposed. They prove the gates
9.230	with fearful care, bind bridge with tower, and bring
9.231	good store of javelins. Serestus bold
9.232	and Mnestheus to their labors promptly fly,
9.233	whom Sire Aeneas bade in time of stress
9.234	to have authority and free command
9.235	over his warriars. Along the walls
9.236	the legions, by the cast of lots, divide
9.237	the pain and peril, giving each his due
9.238	of alternating vigil and repose.
9.239	Nisus kept sentry at the gate: a youth
9.240	of eager heart for noble deeds, the son
9.241	of Hyrtacus, whom in Aeneas' train
9.242	Ida the huntress sent; swift could he speed
9.243	the spear or light-winged arrow to its aim.
9.244	Beside him was Euryalus, his friend:
9.245	of all th' Aeneadae no youth more fair
9.246	wore Trojan arms; upon his cheek unshorn
9.247	the tender bloom of boyhood lingered still.
9.248	Their loving hearts were one, and oft in war
9.249	they battled side by side, as in that hour
9.250	a common sentry at the gate they shared.
9.251	Said Nisus: “Is it gods above that breathe
9.252	this fever in my soul, Euryalus?
9.253	or is the tyrant passion of each breast
9.254	the god it serves? Me now my urgent mind
9.255	to battles or some mighty deed impels,
9.256	and will not give me rest. Look yonder, where
9.257	the Rutuli in dull security
9.258	the siege maintain. Yet are their lights but few.
9.259	They are asleep or drunk, and in their line
9.260	is many a silent space. O, hear my thought,
9.261	and what my heart is pondering. To recall
9.262	Aeneas is the dearest wish to-night
9.263	of all, both high and low. They need true men
9.264	to find him and bring tidings. If our chiefs
9.265	but grant me leave to do the thing I ask
9.266	(Claiming no reward save what honor gives),
9.267	methinks I could search out by yonder hill
9.268	a path to Pallanteum.” The amazed
9.269	Euryalus, flushed warm with eager love
9.270	for deeds of glory, instantly replied
9.271	to his high-hearted friend: “Dost thou refuse,
9.272	my Nisus, to go with me hand in hand
9.273	when mighty deeds are done? Could I behold
9.274	thee venturing alone on danger? Nay!
9.275	Not thus my sire Opheltes, schooled in war,
9.276	taught me his true child, 'mid the woes of Troy
9.277	and Argive terrors reared; not thus with thee
9.278	have I proved craven, since we twain were leal
9.279	to great Aeneas, sharing all his doom.
9.280	In this breast also is a heart which knows
9.281	contempt of life, and deems such deeds, such praise,
9.282	well worth a glorious death.” Nisus to him:
9.283	“I have not doubted thee, nor e'er could have
9.284	one thought disloyal. May almighty Jove,
9.285	or whatsoe'er good power my purpose sees,
9.286	bring me triumphant to thy arms once more!
9.287	But if, as oft in doubtful deeds befalls,
9.288	some stroke of chance, or will divine, should turn
9.289	to adverse, 't is my fondest prayer that thou
9.290	shouldst live the longer of us twain. Thy years
9.291	suit better with more life. Oh! let there be
9.292	one mourner true to carry to its grave
9.293	my corpse, recaptured in the desperate fray,
9.294	or ransomed for a price. Or if this boon
9.295	should be—'t is Fortune's common way—refused,
9.296	then pay the debt of grief and loyal woe
9.297	unto my far-off dust, and garlands leave
9.298	upon an empty tomb. No grief I give
9.299	to any sorrowing mother; one alone,
9.300	of many Trojan mothers, had the heart
9.301	to follow thee, her child, and would not stay
9.302	in great Acestes' land.” His friend replied:
9.303	“Thou weavest but a web of empty words
9.304	and reasons vain, nor dost thou shake at all
9.305	my heart's resolve. Come, let us haste away!”
9.306	He answered so, and summoned to the gate
9.307	a neighboring watch, who, bringing prompt relief,
9.308	the sentry-station took; then quitted he
9.309	his post assigned; at Nisus' side he strode,
9.310	and both impatient sped them to the King.
9.311	Now in all lands all creatures that have breath
9.312	lulled care in slumber, and each heart forgot
9.313	its load of toil and pain. But they who led
9.314	the Teucrian cause, with all their chosen brave,
9.315	took counsel in the kingdom's hour of need
9.316	what action to command or whom dispatch
9.317	with tidings to Aeneas. In mid-camp
9.318	on long spears leaning and with ready shield
9.319	to leftward slung, th' assembled warriors stood.
9.320	Thither in haste arrived the noble pair,
9.321	brave Nisus with Euryalus his friend,
9.322	and craved a hearing, for their suit, they said,
9.323	was urgent and well-worth a patient ear.
9.324	Iulus to the anxious striplings gave
9.325	a friendly welcome, bidding Nisus speak.
9.326	The son of Hyrtacus obeyed: “O, hear,
9.327	Princes of Teucria, with impartial mind,
9.328	nor judge by our unseasoned youth the worth
9.329	of what we bring. Yon Rutule watch is now
9.330	in drunken sleep, and all is silent there.
9.331	With our own eyes we picked out a good place
9.332	to steal a march, that cross-road by the gate
9.333	close-fronting on the bridge. Their lines of fire
9.334	are broken, and a murky, rolling smoke
9.335	fills all the region. If ye grant us leave
9.336	by this good luck to profit, we will find
9.337	Aeneas and the walls of Palatine,
9.338	and after mighty slaughter and huge spoil
9.339	ye soon shall see us back. Nor need ye fear
9.340	we wander from the way. Oft have we seen
9.341	that city's crest loom o'er the shadowy vales,
9.342	where we have hunted all day long and know
9.343	each winding of yon river.” Then uprose
9.344	aged Aletes, crowned with wisdom's years:
9.345	“Gods of our fathers, who forevermore
9.346	watch over Troy, ye surely had no mind
9.347	to blot out Teucria's name, when ye bestowed
9.348	such courage on young hearts, and bade them be
9.349	so steadfast and so leal.” Joyful he clasped
9.350	their hands in his, and on their shoulders leaned,
9.351	his aged cheek and visage wet with tears.
9.352	“What reward worthy of such actions fair,
9.353	dear heroes, could be given? Your brightest prize
9.354	will come from Heaven and your own hearts. The rest
9.355	Aeneas will right soon bestow; nor will
9.356	Ascanius, now in youth's unblemished prime,
9.357	ever forget your praise.” Forthwith replied
9.358	Aeneas' son, “By all our household gods,
9.359	by great Assaracus, and every shrine
9.360	of venerable Vesta, I confide
9.361	my hopes, my fortunes, and all future weal
9.362	to your heroic hearts. O, bring me back
9.363	my father! Set him in these eyes once more!
9.364	That day will tears be dry; and I will give
9.365	two silver wine-cups graven and o'erlaid
9.366	with clear-cut figures, which my father chose
9.367	out of despoiled Arisbe; also two
9.368	full talents of pure gold, and tripods twain,
9.369	and ancient wine-bowl, Tyrian Dido's token.
9.370	But if indeed our destiny shall be
9.371	to vanquish Italy in prosperous war,
9.372	to seize the sceptre and divide the spoil, —
9.373	saw you that steed of Turnus and the arms
9.374	in which he rode, all golden? That same steed,
9.375	that glittering shield and haughty crimson crest
9.376	I will reserve thee, e'er the lots are cast,
9.377	and, Nisus, they are thine. Hereto my sire
9.378	will add twelve captive maids of beauty rare,
9.379	and slaves in armor; last, thou hast the fields
9.380	which now Latinus holds. But as for thee,
9.381	to whom my youth but binds me closer still,
9.382	thee, kingly boy, my whole heart makes my own,
9.383	and through all changeful fortune we shall be
9.384	inseparable peers: nor will I seek
9.385	renown and glory, or in peace or war,
9.386	forgetting thee: but trust thee from this day
9.387	in deed and word.” To him in answer spoke
9.388	euryalus, “O, may no future show
9.389	this heart unworthy thy heroic call!
9.390	And may our fortune ever prosperous prove,
9.391	not adverse. But I now implore of thee
9.392	a single boon worth all beside. I have
9.393	a mother, from the venerated line
9.394	of Priam sprung, whom not the Trojan shore
9.395	nor King Acestes' city could detain,
9.396	alas! from following me. I leave her now
9.397	without farewell; nor is her love aware
9.398	of my supposed peril. For I swear
9.399	by darkness of this night and thy right hand,
9.400	that all my courage fails me if I see
9.401	a mother's tears. O, therefore, I implore,
9.402	be thou her sorrow's comfort and sustain
9.403	her solitary day. Such grace from thee
9.404	equip me for my war, and I shall face
9.405	with braver heart whatever fortune brings.”
9.406	With sudden sorrow thrilled, the veteran lords
9.407	of Teucria showed their tears. But most of all
9.408	such likeness of his own heart's filial love
9.409	on fair Iulus moved, and thus he spoke:
9.410	“Promise thyself what fits thy generous deeds.
9.411	Thy mother shall be mine, Creusa's name
9.412	alone not hers; nor is the womb unblest
9.413	that bore a child like thee. Whate'er success
9.414	may follow, I make oath immutable
9.415	by my own head, on which my father swore,
9.416	that all I promise thee of gift or praise
9.417	if home thou comest triumphing, shall be
9.418	the glory of thy mother and thy kin.”
9.419	Weeping he spoke, and from his shoulder drew
9.420	the golden sword, well-wrought and wonderful,
9.421	which once in Crete Lycaon's cunning made
9.422	and sheathed in ivory. On Nisus then
9.423	Mnestheus bestowed a shaggy mantle torn
9.424	from a slain lion; good Aletes gave
9.425	exchange of crested helms. In such array
9.426	they hastened forth; and all the princely throng,
9.427	young men and old, ran with them to the gates,
9.428	praying all gods to bless. Iulus then,
9.429	a fair youth, but of grave, heroic soul
9.430	beyond his years, gave them in solemn charge
9.431	full many a message for his sire, but these
9.432	the hazard of wild winds soon scattered far,
9.433	and flung them fruitless on the darkening storm.
9.434	Forth through the moat they climb, and steal away
9.435	through midnight shades, to where their foemen lie
9.436	encamped in arms; of whom, before these fall,
9.437	a host shall die. Along the turf were seen,
9.438	laid low in heavy slumber and much wine,
9.439	a prostrate troop; the horseless chariots
9.440	stood tilted on the shore, 'twixt rein and wheel
9.441	the drivers dozed, wine-cups and idle swords
9.442	strewn round them without heed. The first to speak
9.443	was Nisus. “Look, Euryalus,” he cried,
9.444	“Now boldly strike. The hour to do the deed
9.445	is here, the path this way. Keep wide-eyed watch
9.446	that no man smite behind us. I myself
9.447	will mow the mighty fieid, and lead thee on
9.448	in a wide swath of slaughter.” With this word
9.449	he shut his lips; and hurled him with his sword
9.450	on haughty Rhamnes, who lay propped at ease
9.451	on pillows huge, and from his heaving breast
9.452	poured slumber loud: of royal stem was he
9.453	and honored of King Turnus for his skill
9.454	in augury; yet could no augur's charm
9.455	that bloody stroke forefend. And Nisus slew
9.456	three slaves near by, that lay in reckless sleep
9.457	upon their spears; then him that bore the shield
9.458	of Remus, then the driver of his car
9.459	close to the horses caught; his sword cut through
9.460	their prostrate necks; then their great master's head
9.461	he lifted high, and left decapitate
9.462	the huge corpse spilling forth its crimson gore
9.463	o'er couch and ground. Like stroke on Lamus fell
9.464	and Lamyrus, with young Serranus, who
9.465	had gamed the midnight through and sleeping lay,
9.466	his fair young body to the wine-god given;
9.467	but happier now had that long-revelling night
9.468	been merry till the dawn! Thus round full folds
9.469	of sheep a famished lion fiercely prowls;
9.470	mad hunger moves him; he devours and rends
9.471	with bloody, roaring mouth, the feeble flock
9.472	that trembles and is dumb. Nor was the sword
9.473	of fair Euryalus less fatal found;
9.474	but fiercely raging on his path of death,
9.475	he pressed on through a base and nameless throng,
9.476	Rhoetus, Herbesus, Fadus, Abaris;
9.477	surprising all save Rhoetus, who awake
9.478	saw every stroke, and crouched in craven fear
9.479	behind a mighty wine-bowl; but not less
9.480	clean through his bare breast as he started forth
9.481	the youth thrust home his sword, then drew it back
9.482	death-dripping, while the bursting purple stream
9.483	of life outflowed, with mingling blood and wine.
9.484	Then, flushed with stealthy slaughter, he crept near
9.485	the followers of Messapus, where he saw
9.486	their camp-fire dying down, and tethered steeds
9.487	upon the meadow feeding. Nisus then
9.488	knew the hot lust of slaughter had swept on
9.489	too far, and cried, “Hold off! For, lo,
9.490	the monitory dawn is nigh. Revenge
9.491	has fed us to the full. We have achieved
9.492	clean passage through the foe.” Full many a prize
9.493	was left untaken: princely suits of mail
9.494	enwrought with silver pure, huge drinking-bowls,
9.495	and broideries fair. Yet grasped Euryalus
9.496	the blazonry at Rhamnes' corselet hung,
9.497	and belt adorned with gold: which were a gift
9.498	to Remulus of Tibur from the store
9.499	of opulent Caedicus, who sued from far
9.500	to be a friend; and these in death he gave
9.501	to his son's son, who slain in battle fell,
9.502	and proud Rutulians seized them with the spoil.
9.503	Euryalus about his shoulder strong
9.504	this booty slung—unprofitable gain! —
9.505	and fitted on a gorgeous, crested helm
9.506	which once Messapus wore. So from the camp,
9.507	escaping danger, the two champions ran.
9.508	But horsemen from the Latin city sent
9.509	to join the serried legions of the plain
9.510	had come at Turnus' call, three hundred strong
9.511	all bearing shields, and under the command
9.512	of Volscens. Nigh the camp and walls they drew;
9.513	and soon they spied upon the leftward path
9.514	th' heroic pair, where in dim shades of night
9.515	the helmet of Euryalus betrayed
9.516	the heedless boy, and with a glancing beam
9.517	flashed on the foe. Nor was it seen in vain.
9.518	Loud from the line the voice of Volscens called:
9.519	“Stand, gentlemen! What business brings you here?
9.520	Whose your allegiance? Whither speed so fast?”
9.521	No answer gave they save to fly in haste
9.522	to cover of the forest and deep gloom
9.523	of the defensive night. The horsemen then
9.524	blocked every crossway known, and, scattering wide,
9.525	kept sentry at the entrance. The great wood
9.526	was all of tangled brush and blinding shade
9.527	of flex-boughs. Impenetrable thorns
9.528	had thickly overgrown, and seldom showed
9.529	a pathway through the maze. Euryalus,
9.530	by the black branches and his ponderous spoil
9.531	impeded, groped along in fearful doubt,
9.532	deceived and quite astray. Nisus his friend
9.533	had quit him, and incautiously had forced
9.534	a sally through the close-encircling foe,
9.535	into that region which should after bear
9.536	the name of Alba—a rude shelter then
9.537	for King Latinus' herds. He stayed him there
9.538	and looked, but vainly, for the comrade gone.
9.539	“Euryalus, ill-fated boy!” he cried,
9.540	“Where have I lost thee in the pathless wild?
9.541	How find thee? How retrace the blinding maze
9.542	of yonder treacherous wood?” Yet ere he said,
9.543	on his own path he turns him back, and scans
9.544	his own light footprints through the tangled thorn,
9.545	so dark and still. But suddenly he hears
9.546	the tread of horses, with confusing din
9.547	and tumult of pursuit. Nor was it long
9.548	he tarried ere upon his anguished ear
9.549	smote a great cry: and, lo! Euryalus,
9.550	trapped by the dark night, the deceptive ground,
9.551	faced the whole onset, and fell back o'erwhelmed
9.552	by a loud mob of foes, while his sole sword
9.553	tried many a thrust in vain. O, what defence
9.554	may Nisus bring? With what audacious arms
9.555	his chosen comrade save? Shall he make bare
9.556	his dying breast to all their swords, and run
9.557	to honorable death that bloody way?
9.558	he swung his spear with lifted arm, then looked
9.559	to the still moon, in heaven, and thus implored:
9.560	“O goddess, aid me in my evil case.
9.561	O glory of the stars, Latona's child!
9.562	O guardian of groves, if in my name
9.563	my father Hyrtacus made offerings
9.564	on burning altars, if my own right hand,
9.565	successful in the chase, ere hung its gift
9.566	beneath thy dome or on thy sacred wall,
9.567	grant me yon troop to scatter. Guide my spear
9.568	along its path in air.” He spoke, and hurled
9.569	with all his gathered strength the shaft of steel.
9.570	the swift spear clove the shades of night, and struck
9.571	full in the back of Sulmo, where it split,
9.572	but tore through to his very heart. The breast
9.573	poured forth life's glowing stream, and he, o'erthrown
9.574	lay cold in death, while his huge, heaving sides
9.575	gave lingering throes. The men about him stared
9.576	this way and that. But Nisus, fiercer still,
9.577	poised level with his ear a second shaft,
9.578	and, while the foeman paused, the whizzing spear
9.579	straight through the brows of Tagus drove, and clung
9.580	deep in the cloven brain. In frenzy rose
9.581	Volscens, but nowhere could espy what hand
9.582	the shaft had hurled, nor whither his wild rage
9.583	could make reply. “But thou,” he cried, “shalt feed
9.584	with thy hot blood my honor and revenge
9.585	for both the slain.” Then with a sword unsheathed
9.586	upon Euryalus he fell. Loud shrieked
9.587	Nisus, of reason reft, who could not bear
9.588	such horror, nor in sheltering gloom of night
9.589	longer abide: “'T is I, 't is I!” he said.
9.590	look on the man who slew them! Draw on me
9.591	your swords, Rutulians! The whole stratagem
9.592	was mine, mine only, and the lad ye slay
9.593	dared not, and could not. O, by Heaven above
9.594	and by the all-beholding stars I swear,
9.595	he did but love his hapless friend too well.”
9.596	But while he spoke, the furious-thrusting sword
9.597	had pierced the tender body, and run through
9.598	the bosom white as snow. Euryalus
9.599	sank prone in death; upon his goodly limbs
9.600	the life-blood ran unstopped, and low inclined
9.601	the drooping head; as when some purpled flower,
9.602	cut by the ploughshare, dies, or poppies proud
9.603	with stem forlorn their ruined beauty bow
9.604	before the pelting storm. Then Nisus flew
9.605	straight at his foes; but in their throng would find
9.606	Volscens alone, for none but Volscens stayed:
9.607	they gathered thickly round and grappled him
9.608	in shock of steel with steel. But on he plunged,
9.609	swinging in ceaseless circles round his head
9.610	his lightning-sword, and thrust it through the face
9.611	of shrieking Volscens, with his own last breath
9.612	striking his foeman down; then cast himself
9.613	upon his fallen comrade's breast; and there,
9.614	stabbed through, found tranquil death and sure repose.
9.615	Heroic pair and blest! If aught I sing
9.616	have lasting music, no remotest age
9.617	shall blot your names from honor's storied scroll:
9.618	not while the altars of Aeneas' line
9.619	shall crown the Capitol's unshaken hill,
9.620	nor while the Roman Father's hand sustains
9.621	its empire o'er the world.
9.622	The Rutules seized the spoils of victory,
9.623	and slowly to their camp, with wail and cry,
9.624	bore Volscens' corse; and in the eamp they made
9.625	like wailing over Rhamnes lifeless found,
9.626	o'er Numa and Serranus, and a throng
9.627	of princes dead. The gazing people pressed
9.628	around the slain, the dying, where the earth
9.629	ran red with slaughter and full many a stream
9.630	of trickling gore; nor did they fail to know
9.631	Messapus' glittering helm, his baldric fair,
9.632	recaptured now with lavish sweat and pain.
9.633	Now, from Tithonus' saffron couch set free,
9.634	Aurora over many a land outpoured
9.635	the rising morn; the sun's advancing beam
9.636	unveiled the world; and Turnus to his host
9.637	gave signal to stand forth, while he arrayed
9.638	himself in glorious arms. Then every chief
9.639	awoke his mail-clad company, and stirred
9.640	their slumbering wrath with tidings from the foe.
9.641	Tumultuously shouting, they impaled
9.642	on lifted spears—O pitiable sight! —
9.643	the heads of Nisus and Euryalus.
9.644	Th' undaunted Trojans stood in battle-line
9.645	along the wall to leftward (for the right
9.646	the river-front defended) keeping guard
9.647	on the broad moat; upon the ramparts high
9.648	sad-eyed they stood, and shuddered as they saw
9.649	the hero-faces thrust aloft; too well
9.650	their loyal grief the blood-stained features knew.
9.651	On restless pinions to the trembling town
9.652	had voiceful Rumor hied, and to the ears
9.653	of that lone mother of Euryalus
9.654	relentless flown. Through all her feeble frame
9.655	the chilling sorrow sped. From both her hands
9.656	dropped web and shuttle; she flew shrieking forth,
9.657	ill-fated mother! and with tresses torn,
9.658	to the wide ramparts and the battle-line
9.659	ran frantic, heeding naught of men-at-arms,
9.660	nor peril nor the rain of falling spears;
9.661	and thus with loud and lamentable cry
9.662	filled all the air: “Is it in yonder guise,
9.663	Euryalus, thou comest? Art thou he,
9.664	last comfort of my life? O cruel one!
9.665	Couldst thou desert me? When they thrust thee forth
9.666	to death and danger, did they dare refuse
9.667	a wretched mother's last embrace? But now —
9.668	O woe is me!—upon this alien shore
9.669	thou liest for a feast to Latin dogs
9.670	and carrion birds. Nor did thy mother lead
9.671	the mourners to thy grave, nor shut those eyes,
9.672	nor wash the dreadful wounds, nor cover thee
9.673	with the fair shroud, which many a night and day
9.674	I swiftly wove, and at my web and loom
9.675	forgot my years and sorrows. Whither now
9.676	to seek and follow thee? What spot of earth
9.677	holds the torn body and the mangled limbs?
9.678	Is all the gift thou bringest home, dear child,
9.679	this? O, was this the prize for which I came
9.680	o'er land and sea? O, stab me very deep,
9.681	if ye have any pity; hurl on me
9.682	your every spear, Rutulians; make of me
9.683	your swords' first work. Or, Father of the gods!
9.684	Show mercy, thou! and with thy lightning touch
9.685	this head accurst, and let it fall by thee
9.686	down to the dark. For else what power is mine
9.687	my tortured life to end?” Her agony
9.688	smote on their listening souls; a wail of woe
9.689	along the concourse ran. Stern men-at-arms
9.690	felt valor for a moment sleep, and all
9.691	their rage of battle fail. But while she stirred
9.692	the passion of her grief, Ilioneus
9.693	and young Iulus, weeping filial tears,
9.694	bade Actor and Idaeus, lifting her
9.695	in both their reverent arms, to bear her home.
9.696	But now the brazen trumpet's fearsome song
9.697	blares loud, and startled shouts of soldiery
9.698	spread through the roaring sky. The Volscian band
9.699	press to the siege, and, locking shield with shield,
9.700	fill the great trenches, tear the palisades,
9.701	or seek approach by ladders up the walls,
9.702	where'er the line of the defenders thins, and light
9.703	through their black circle shines. The Trojans pour
9.704	promiscuous missiles down, and push out hard
9.705	with heavy poles—so well have they been schooled
9.706	to fight against long sieges. They fling down
9.707	a crushing weight of rocks, in hope to break
9.708	th' assailing line, where roofed in serried shields
9.709	the foe each charge repels. But not for long
9.710	the siegers stand; along their dense array
9.711	the crafty Teucrians down the rampart roll
9.712	a boulder like a hill-top, laying low
9.713	the Rutule troop and crashing through their shields.
9.714	Nor may the bold Rutulian longer hope
9.715	to keep in cover, but essays to storm
9.716	only with far-flung shafts the bastion strong.
9.717	Here grim Mezentius, terrible to see,
9.718	waved an Etrurian pine, and made his war
9.719	with smoking firebrands; there, in equal rage,
9.720	Messapus, the steed-tamer, Neptune's son,
9.721	ripped down the palisade, and at the breach
9.722	strung a steep path of ladders up the wall.
9.723	Aid, O Calliope, the martial song!
9.724	Tell me what carnage and how many deaths
9.725	the sword of Turnus wrought: what peer in arms
9.726	each hero to the world of ghosts sent down.
9.727	Unroll the war's great book before these eyes.
9.728	A tower was there, well-placed and looming large,
9.729	with many a lofty bridge, which desperately
9.730	th' Italians strove to storm, and strangely plied
9.731	besieging enginery to cast it down:
9.732	the Trojans hurled back stones, or, standing close,
9.733	flung through the loopholes a swift shower of spears.
9.734	But Turnus launched a firebrand, and pierced
9.735	the wooden wall with flame, which in the wind
9.736	leaped larger, and devoured from floor to floor,
9.737	burning each beam away. The trembling guards
9.738	sought flight in vain; and while they crowded close
9.739	into the side unkindled yet, the tower
9.740	bowed its whole weight and fell, with sudden crash
9.741	that thundered through the sky. Along the ground
9.742	half dead the warriors fell (the crushing mass
9.743	piled over them) by their own pointed spears
9.744	pierced to the heart, or wounded mortally
9.745	by cruel splinters of the wreck. Two men,
9.746	Helenor one, and Lyeus at his side,
9.747	alone get free. Helenor of the twain
9.748	was a mere youth; the slave Lycymnia
9.749	bore him in secret to the Lydian King,
9.750	and, arming him by stealth, had sent away
9.751	to serve the Trojan cause. One naked sword
9.752	for arms had he, and on his virgin shield
9.753	no blazon of renown; but when he saw
9.754	the hosts of Turnus front him, and the lines
9.755	this way and that of Latins closing round, —
9.756	as a fierce, forest-creature, brought to bay
9.757	in circling pack of huntsmen, shows its teeth
9.758	against the naked spears, and scorning death
9.759	leaps upward on the javelins,—even so,
9.760	not loth to die, the youthful soldier flew
9.761	straight at the centre of his foes, and where
9.762	the shining swords looked thickest, there he sprung.
9.763	But Lyeus, swifter-footed, forced his way
9.764	past the opposing spears and made escape
9.765	far as the ciity-wall, where he would fain
9.766	clutch at the coping and climb up to clasp
9.767	some friend above: but Turnus, spear in hand,
9.768	had hotly followed, and exulting loud
9.769	thus taunted him, “Hadst thou the hope, rash fool,
9.770	beyond this grasp to fly?” So, as he clung,
9.771	he tore him down; and with him broke and fell
9.772	a huge piece of the wall: not otherwise
9.773	a frail hare, or a swan of snow-white wing,
9.774	is clutched in eagle-talons, when the bird
9.775	of Jove soars skyward with his prey; or tender lamb
9.776	from bleating mother and the broken fold
9.777	is stolen by the wolf of Mars. Wild shouts
9.778	on every side resound. In closer siege
9.779	the foe press on, and heap the trenches full,
9.780	or hurl hot-flaming torches at the towers.
9.781	Ilioneus with mountain-mass of stone
9.782	struck down Lucetius, as he crept with fire
9.783	too near the city-gate. Emathion fell
9.784	by Liger's hand, and Corynteus' death
9.785	Asilas dealt: one threw the javelin well;
9.786	th' insidious arrow was Asilas' skill.
9.787	Ortygius was slain by Caeneus, then
9.788	victorious Geneus fell by Turnus' ire.
9.789	Then smote he Dioxippus, and laid low
9.790	Itys and Promolus and Sagaris
9.791	and Clonius, and from the lofty tower
9.792	shot Idas down. The shaft of Capys pierced
9.793	Privernus, whom Themilla's javelin
9.794	but now had lightly grazed, and he, too bold,
9.795	casting his shield far from him, had outspread
9.796	his left hand on the wound: then sudden flew
9.797	the feathered arrow, and the hand lay pinned
9.798	against his left side, while the fatal barb
9.799	was buried in his breathing life. The son
9.800	of Arcens now stood forth in glittering arms.
9.801	His broidered cloak was red Iberian stain,
9.802	and beautiful was he. Arcens his sire
9.803	had sent him to the war; but he was bred
9.804	in a Sicilian forest by a stream
9.805	to his nymph-mother dear, where rose the shrine
9.806	of merciful Palicus, blest and fair.
9.807	But, lo! Mezentius his spear laid by,
9.808	and whirled three times about his head the thong
9.809	of his loud sling: the leaden bullet clove
9.810	the youth's mid-forehead, and his towering form
9.811	fell prostrate its full length along the ground.
9.812	'T was then Ascanius first shot forth in war
9.813	the arrow swift from which all creatures wild
9.814	were wont to fly in fear: and he struck down
9.815	with artful aim Numanus, sturdy foe,
9.816	called Remulus, who lately was espoused
9.817	to Turnus' younger sister. He had stalked
9.818	before the van, and made vociferous noise
9.819	of truths and falsehoods foul and base, his heart
9.820	puffed up with new-found greatness. Up and down
9.821	he strode, and swelled his folly with loud words:
9.822	“No shame have ye this second time to stay
9.823	cooped close within a rampart's craven siege,
9.824	O Phrygians twice-vanquished? Is a wall
9.825	your sole defence from death? Are such the men
9.826	who ask our maids in marriage? Say what god,
9.827	what doting madness, rather, drove ye here
9.828	to Italy? This way ye will not find
9.829	the sons of Atreus nor the trickster tongue
9.830	of voluble Ulysses. Sturdy stock
9.831	are we; our softest new-born babes we dip
9.832	in chilling rivers, till they bear right well
9.833	the current's bitter cold. Our slender lads
9.834	hunt night and day and rove the woods at large,
9.835	or for their merriment break stubborn steeds,
9.836	or bend the horn-tipped bow. Our manly prime
9.837	in willing labor lives, and is inured
9.838	to poverty and scantness; we subdue
9.839	our lands with rake and mattock, or in war
9.840	bid strong-walled cities tremble. Our whole life
9.841	is spent in use of iron; and we goad
9.842	the flanks of bullocks with a javelin's end.
9.843	Nor doth old age, arriving late, impair
9.844	our brawny vigor, nor corrupt the soul
9.845	to frail decay. But over silvered brows
9.846	we bind the helmet. Our unfailing joy
9.847	is rapine, and to pile the plunder high.
9.848	But ye! your gowns-are saffron needlework
9.849	or Tyrian purple; ye love shameful ease,
9.850	or dancing revelry. Your tunics fiow
9.851	long-sleeved, and ye have soft caps ribbon-bound.
9.852	Aye, Phrygian girls are ye, not Phrygian men!
9.853	Hence to your hill of Dindymus! Go hear
9.854	the twy-mouthed piping ye have loved so long.
9.855	The timbrel, hark! the Berecynthian flute
9.856	calls you away, and Ida's goddess calls.
9.857	Leave arms to men, true men! and quit the sword!”
9.858	Of such loud insolence and words of shame
9.859	Ascanius brooked no more, but laid a shaft
9.860	athwart his bowstring, and with arms stretched wide
9.861	took aim, first offering suppliant vow to Jove:
9.862	“Almighty Jupiter, thy favor show
9.863	to my bold deed! So to thy shrine I bear
9.864	gifts year by year, and to thine altars lead
9.865	a bull with gilded brows, snow-white, and tall
9.866	as his own dam, what time his youth begins
9.867	to lower his horns and fling the sand in air.”
9.868	The Father heard, and from a cloudless sky
9.869	thundered to leftward, while the deadly bow
9.870	resounded and the arrow's fearful song
9.871	hissed from the string; it struck unswervingly
9.872	the head of Remulus and clove its way
9.873	deep in the hollows of his brow. “Begone!
9.874	Proud mocker at the brave! Lo, this reply
9.875	twice-vanquished Phrygians to Rutulia send.”
9.876	Ascanius said no more. The Teucrians
9.877	with deep-voiced shout of joy applaud, and lift
9.878	their exultation starward. Then from heaven
9.879	the flowing-haired Apollo bent his gaze
9.880	upon Ausonia's host, and cloud-enthroned
9.881	looked downward o'er the city, speaking thus
9.882	to fair Iulus in his victory:
9.883	“Hail to thy maiden prowess, boy! This way
9.884	the starward path to dwelling-place divine.
9.885	O sired of gods and sire of gods to come,
9.886	all future storms of war by Fate ordained
9.887	shall into peace and lawful calm subside
9.888	beneath the offspring of Assaracus.
9.889	No Trojan destinies thy glory bound.”
9.890	So saying, from his far, ethereal seat
9.891	he hied him down, and, cleaving the quick winds
9.892	drew near Ascanius. He wore the guise
9.893	of aged Butes, who erewhile had borne
9.894	Anchises, armor and kept trusty guard
9.895	before his threshold, but attended now
9.896	Ascanius, by commandment of his sire.
9.897	Clad in this graybeard's every aspect, moved
9.898	apollo forth,—his very voice and hue,
9.899	his hoary locks and grimly sounding shield, —
9.900	and to the flushed Iulus spoke this word:
9.901	“Child of Aeneas, be content that now
9.902	Numanus unavenged thine arrows feels.
9.903	Such dawn of glory great Apollo's will
9.904	concedes, nor envies thee the fatal shaft
9.905	so like his own. But, tender youth, refrain
9.906	hereafter from this war!” So said divine
9.907	Apollo, who, while yet he spoke, put by
9.908	his mortal aspect, and before their eyes
9.909	melted to viewless air. The Teucrians knew
9.910	the vocal god with armament divine
9.911	of arrows; for his rattling quiver smote
9.912	their senses as he fled. Obedient
9.913	to Phoebus' voice they held back from the fray
9.914	Iulus' fury, and their eager souls
9.915	faced the fresh fight and danger's darkest frown.
9.916	From tower to tower along the bastioned wall
9.917	their war-cry flew: they bend with busy hand
9.918	the cruel bow, or swing the whirling thong
9.919	of javelins. The earth on every side
9.920	is strewn with spent shafts, the reverberant shield
9.921	and hollow helmet ring with blows; the fight
9.922	more fiercely swells; not less the bursting storm
9.923	from watery Kid-stars in the western sky
9.924	lashes the plain, or multitudinous hail
9.925	beats upon shallow seas, when angry Jove
9.926	flings forth tempestuous and-boundless rain,
9.927	and splits the bellied clouds in darkened air.
9.928	The brothers Pandarus and Bitias,
9.929	of whom Alcanor was the famous sire,
9.930	on Ida born, and whom Iaera bred
9.931	in sacred wood of Jove, an oread she,
9.932	twin warriors, like their native hills and trees
9.933	of stature proud, now burst those portals wide
9.934	to them in ward consigned, and sword in hand
9.935	challenge the foe to enter. Side by side,
9.936	steel-clad, their tall heads in bright crested helms,
9.937	to left and right, like towers, the champions stand
9.938	as when to skyward, by the gliding waves
9.939	of gentle Athesis or Padus wide,
9.940	a pair of oaks uprise, and lift in air
9.941	their shaggy brows and nodding crests sublime.
9.942	In burst the Rutules where the onward way
9.943	seemed open wide; Quercens no tarrying knows,
9.944	nor proud Aquiculus in well-wrought arms;
9.945	Tmarus sweeps on impetuous, and the host
9.946	of Haemon, child of Mars. Some routed fly;
9.947	some lay their lives-down at the gate. Wild rage
9.948	o'erflows each martial breast, and gathered fast
9.949	the Trojans rally to one point, and dare
9.950	close conflict, or long sallies o'er the plain.
9.951	To Turnus, who upon a distant field
9.952	was storming with huge havoc, came the news
9.953	that now his foe, before a gate thrown wide,
9.954	was red with slaughter. His own fight he stays,
9.955	and speeds him, by enormous rage thrust on,
9.956	to those proud brethren at the Dardan wall.
9.957	There first Antiphates, who made his war
9.958	far in the van (a Theban captive's child
9.959	to great Sarpedon out of wedlock born),
9.960	he felled to earth with whirling javelin:
9.961	th' Italic shaft of cornel lightly flew
9.962	along the yielding air, and through his throat
9.963	pierced deep into the breast; a gaping wound
9.964	gushed blood; the hot shaft to his bosom clung.
9.965	Then Erymas and Merops his strong hand
9.966	laid low: Aphidnus next, then came the turn
9.967	of Bitias, fiery-hearted, furious-eyed:
9.968	but not by javelin,—such cannot fall
9.969	by flying javelin,—the ponderous beam
9.970	of a phalaric spear, with mighty roar,
9.971	like thunderbolt upon him fell; such shock
9.972	neither the bull's-hides of his double shield
9.973	nor twofold corselet's golden scales could stay
9.974	but all his towering frame in ruin fell.
9.975	Earth groaned, and o'er him rang his ample shield.
9.976	so crashes down from Baiae's storied shore
9.977	a rock-built mole, whose mighty masonry,
9.978	piled up with care, men cast into the sea;
9.979	it trails its wreckage far, and fathoms down
9.980	lies broken in the shallows, while the waves
9.981	whirl every way, and showers of black sand
9.982	are scattered on the air: with thunder-sound
9.983	steep Prochyta is shaken, and that bed
9.984	of cruel stone, Inarime, which lies
9.985	heaped o'er Typhoeus by revenge of Jove.
9.986	Now to the Latins Mars, the lord of war,
9.987	gave might and valor, and to their wild hearts
9.988	his spur applied, but on the Teucrians breathed
9.989	dark fear and flight. From every quarter came
9.990	auxiliar hosts, where'er the conflict called,
9.991	and in each bosom pulsed the god of war.
9.992	When Pandarus now saw his brother's corse
9.993	low Iying, and which way the chance and tide
9.994	of battle ran, he violently moved
9.995	the swinging hinges of the gate, and strained
9.996	with both his shoulders broad. He shut outside
9.997	not few of his own people, left exposed
9.998	in fiercest fight but others with himself
9.999	he barred inside and saved them as they fled;
9.1000	nor noted, madman, how the Rutule King
9.1001	had burst in midmost of the line, and now
9.1002	stood prisoned in their wall, as if he were
9.1003	some monstrous tiger among helpless kine.
9.1004	His eyeballs strangely glared; his armor rang
9.1005	terrific, his tall crest shook o'er his brows
9.1006	blood-red, and lightnings glittered from his shield
9.1007	familiar loomed that countenance abhorred
9.1008	and frame gigantic on the shrinking eyes
9.1009	of the Aeneadae. Then Pandarus
9.1010	sprang towering forth, all fever to revenge
9.1011	his brother's slaughter. “Not this way,” he cried
9.1012	“Amata's marriage-gift! No Ardea here
9.1013	mews Turnus in his fathers' halls. Behold
9.1014	thy foeman's castle! Thou art not allowed
9.1015	to take thy leave.” But Turnus looked his way,
9.1016	and smiled with heart unmoved. “Begin! if thou
9.1017	hast manhood in thee, and meet steel with steel!
9.1018	Go tell dead Priam thou discoverest here
9.1019	Achilles!” For reply, the champion tall
9.1020	hurled with his might and main along the air
9.1021	his spear of knotted wood and bark untrimmed.
9.1022	But all it wounded was the passing wind,
9.1023	for Saturn's daughter turned its course awry,
9.1024	and deep in the great gate the spear-point drove.
9.1025	“Now from the stroke this right arm means for thee
9.1026	thou shalt not fly. Not such the sender of
9.1027	this weapon and this wound.” He said, and towered
9.1028	aloft to his full height; the lifted sword
9.1029	clove temples, brows, and beardless cheeks clean through
9.1030	with loudly ringing blow; the ground beneath
9.1031	shook with the giant's ponderous fall, and, lo,
9.1032	with nerveless limbs, and brains spilt o'er his shield,
9.1033	dead on the earth he lay! in equal halves
9.1034	the sundered head from either shoulder swung.
9.1035	In horror and amaze the Trojans all
9.1036	dispersed and fled; had but the conqueror thought
9.1037	to break the barriers of the gates and call
9.1038	his followers through, that fatal day had seen
9.1039	an ending of the Teucrians and their war.
9.1040	But frenzied joy of slaughter urged him on,
9.1041	infuriate, to smite the scattering foe.
9.1042	First Phaleris he caught; then cut the knees
9.1043	of Gyges; both their spears he snatched away
9.1044	and hurled them at the rout; 't was Juno roused
9.1045	his utmost might of rage. Now Halys fell,
9.1046	and Phegeus, whom he pierced right through the shield:
9.1047	next, at the walls and urging reckless war,
9.1048	Alcander, Halius, and Noemon gave
9.1049	their lives, and Prytanis went down. In vain
9.1050	Lynceus made stand and called his comrades brave:
9.1051	for Turnus from the right with waving sword
9.1052	caught at him and lopped off with one swift blow
9.1053	the head, which with its helmet rolled away.
9.1054	Next Amycus, destroyer of wild beasts,
9.1055	who knew full well to smear a crafty barb
9.1056	with venomed oil; young Clytius he slew,
9.1057	son of the wind-god; then on Cretheus fell,
9.1058	a follower of the muses and their friend:
9.1059	Cretheus, whose every joy it was to sing,
9.1060	and fit his numbers to the chorded Iyre;
9.1061	steeds, wars, armed men were his perpetual song.
9.1062	At last the Teucrian chiefs had heard the tale
9.1063	of so much slaughter; and in council met
9.1064	are Mnestheus and Serestus bold, who see
9.1065	their comrades routed and the conquering foe
9.1066	within the gates. Cries Mnestheus, “Whither fly?
9.1067	What open way is yonder or what wall?
9.1068	Beyond these ramparts lost what stronger lie?
9.1069	Shall one lone man here in your walls confined,
9.1070	make havoc unavenged and feed the grave
9.1071	with your best warriors? 0 cowards vile!
9.1072	For your sad country and her ancient gods
9.1073	and for renowned Aeneas, can ye feel
9.1074	no pity and no shame?” Enflamed to fight
9.1075	by words like these, they close the line, and stand
9.1076	in strong array. So Turnus for a space
9.1077	out of the battle step by step withdrew
9.1078	to make the river-bank his rearguard strong;
9.1079	whereat the Teucrians, shouting loud, swept on
9.1080	the fiercer, and in solid mass pressed round.
9.1081	as when a troop of hunters with keen spears
9.1082	encircle a wild lion, who in fear,
9.1083	but glaring grim and furious, backward falls,
9.1084	valor and rage constrain him ne'er to cease
9.1085	fronting the foe; yet not for all his ire
9.1086	can he against such serried steel make way:
9.1087	so Turnus backward with a lingering step
9.1088	unwilling drew, and wrath his heart oterflowed.
9.1089	for twice already had he cloven a path
9.1090	into the foe's mid-press, and twice had driven
9.1091	their flying lines in panic through the town.
9.1092	But now the whole throng from the camp he sees
9.1093	massed to the onset. Nor will Juno now
9.1094	dare give him vigor to withstand, for Jove
9.1095	had sent aerial Iris out of heaven
9.1096	with stern commandment to his sister-queen
9.1097	that Turnus from the Teucrian walls retire.
9.1098	Therefore the warrior's shield avails no more,
9.1099	nor his strong arm; but he is overthrown
9.1100	by general assault. Around his brows
9.1101	his smitten helmet rings; the ponderous mail
9.1102	cracks under falling stones; the haughty plumes
9.1103	are scattered from his head, nor can the boss
9.1104	of his stout shield endure; the Trojans hurl
9.1105	redoubled rain of spears; and with them speeds
9.1106	Mnestheus like thunderbolt. The hero's flesh
9.1107	dissolves in sweat; no room to breathe has he;
9.1108	his limbs are spent and weary; his whole frame
9.1109	shakes with his gasping breath: then bounding fort
9.1110	with all his harness on, headlong he plunged
9.1111	into the flowing stream; its yellow tide
9.1112	embraced him as he fell, and gentle waves
9.1113	restored him smiling to his friends in arms,
9.1114	with all the gore and carnage washed away.
10.1	Meanwhile Olympus, seat of sovereign sway,
10.2	threw wide its portals, and in conclave fair
10.3	the Sire of gods and King of all mankind
10.4	summoned th' immortals to his starry court,
10.5	whence, high-enthroned, the spreading earth he views—
10.6	and Teucria's camp and Latium's fierce array.
10.7	Beneath the double-gated dome the gods
10.8	were sitting; Jove himself the silence broke:
10.9	“O people of Olympus, wherefore change
10.10	your purpose and decree, with partial minds
10.11	in mighty strife contending? I refused
10.12	such clash of war 'twixt Italy and Troy.
10.13	Whence this forbidden feud? What fears
10.14	seduced to battles and injurious arms
10.15	either this folk or that? Th' appointed hour
10.16	for war shall be hereafter—speed it not!—
10.17	When cruel Carthage to the towers of Rome
10.18	shall bring vast ruin, streaming fiercely down
10.19	the opened Alp. Then hate with hate shall vie,
10.20	and havoc have no bound. Till then, give o'er,
10.21	and smile upon the concord I decree!”
10.22	Thus briefly, Jove. But golden Venus made
10.23	less brief reply. “O Father, who dost hold
10.24	o'er Man and all things an immortal sway!
10.25	Of what high throne may gods the aid implore
10.26	save thine? Behold of yonder Rutuli
10.27	th' insulting scorn! Among them Turnus moves
10.28	in chariot proud, and boasts triumphant war
10.29	in mighty words. Nor do their walls defend
10.30	my Teucrians now. But in their very gates,
10.31	and on their mounded ramparts, in close fight
10.32	they breast their foes and fill the moats with blood.
10.33	Aeneas knows not, and is far away.
10.34	Will ne'er the siege have done? A second time
10.35	above Troy's rising walls the foe impends;
10.36	another host is gathered, and once more
10.37	from his Aetolian Arpi wrathful speeds
10.38	a Diomed. I doubt not that for me
10.39	wounds are preparing. Yea, thy daughter dear
10.40	awaits a mortal sword! If by thy will
10.41	unblest and unapproved the Trojans came
10.42	to Italy, for such rebellious crime
10.43	give them their due, nor lend them succor, thou,
10.44	with thy strong hand! But if they have obeyed
10.45	unnumbered oracles from gods above
10.46	and sacred shades below, who now has power
10.47	to thwart thy bidding, or to weave anew
10.48	the web of Fate? Why speak of ships consumed
10.49	along my hallowed Erycinian shore?
10.50	Or of the Lord of Storms, whose furious blasts
10.51	were summoned from Aeolia? Why tell
10.52	of Iris sped from heaven? Now she moves
10.53	the region of the shades (one kingdom yet
10.54	from her attempt secure) and thence lets loose
10.55	Alecto on the world above, who strides
10.56	in frenzied wrath along th' Italian hills.
10.57	No more my heart now cherishes its hope
10.58	of domination, though in happier days
10.59	such was thy promise. Let the victory fall
10.60	to victors of thy choice! If nowhere lies
10.61	the land thy cruel Queen would deign accord
10.62	unto the Teucrian people,—O my sire,
10.63	I pray thee by yon smouldering wreck of Troy
10.64	to let Ascanius from the clash of arms
10.65	escape unscathed. Let my own offspring live!
10.66	Yea, let Aeneas, tossed on seas unknown,
10.67	find some chance way; let my right hand avail
10.68	to shelter him and from this fatal war
10.69	in safety bring. For Amathus is mine,
10.70	mine are Cythera and the Paphian hills
10.71	and temples in Idalium. Let him drop
10.72	the sword, and there live out inglorious days.
10.73	By thy decree let Carthage overwhelm
10.74	Ausonia's power; nor let defence be found
10.75	to stay the Tyrian arms! What profits it
10.76	that he escaped the wasting plague of war
10.77	and fled Argolic fires? or that he knew
10.78	so many perils of wide wilderness
10.79	and waters rude? The Teucrians seek in vain
10.80	new-born Troy in Latium. Better far
10.81	crouched on their country's ashes to abide,
10.82	and keep that spot of earth where once was Troy!
10.83	Give back, O Father, I implore thee, give
10.84	Xanthus and Simois back! Let Teucer's sons
10.85	unfold once more the tale of Ilium's woe!”
10.86	Then sovereign Juno, flushed with solemn scorn,
10.87	made answer. “Dost thou bid me here profane
10.88	the silence of my heart, and gossip forth
10.89	of secret griefs? What will of god or man
10.90	impelled Aeneas on his path of war,
10.91	or made him foeman of the Latin King?
10.92	Fate brought him to Italia? Be it so!
10.93	Cassandra's frenzy he obeyed. What voice —
10.94	say, was it mine?—urged him to quit his camp,
10.95	risk life in storms, or trust his war, his walls,
10.96	to a boy-captain, or stir up to strife
10.97	Etruria's faithful, unoffending sons?
10.98	What god, what pitiless behest of mine,
10.99	impelled him to such harm? Who traces here
10.100	the hand of Juno, or of Iris sped
10.101	from heaven? Is it an ignoble stroke
10.102	that Italy around the new-born Troy
10.103	makes circling fire, and Turnus plants his heel
10.104	on his hereditary earth, the son
10.105	of old Pilumnus and the nymph divine,
10.106	Venilia? For what offence would Troy
10.107	bring sword and fire on Latium, or enslave
10.108	lands of an alien name, and bear away
10.109	plunder and spoil? Why seek they marriages,
10.110	and snatch from arms of love the plighted maids?
10.111	An olive-branch is in their hands; their ships
10.112	make menace of grim steel. Thy power one day
10.113	ravished Aeneas from his Argive foes,
10.114	and gave them shape of cloud and fleeting air
10.115	to strike at for a man. Thou hast transformed
10.116	his ships to daughters of the sea. What wrong
10.117	if I, not less, have lent the Rutuli
10.118	something of strength in war? Aeneas, then,
10.119	is far away and knows not! Far away
10.120	let him remain, not knowing! If thou sway'st
10.121	Cythera, Paphos, and Idalium,
10.122	why rouse a city pregnant with loud wars,
10.123	and fiery hearts provoke? That fading power
10.124	of Phrygia, do I, forsooth, essay
10.125	to ruin utterly? O, was it I
10.126	exposed ill-fated Troy to Argive foe?
10.127	For what offence in vast array of arms
10.128	did Europe rise and Asia, for a rape
10.129	their peace dissolving? Was it at my word
10.130	th' adulterous Dardan shepherd came to storm
10.131	the Spartan city? Did my hand supply
10.132	his armament, or instigate a war
10.133	for Cupid's sake? Then was thy decent hour
10.134	to tremble for thy children; now too late
10.135	the folly of thy long lament to Heaven,
10.136	and objurgation vain.” Such Juno's plea;
10.137	the throng of gods with voices loud or low
10.138	gave various reply: as gathering winds
10.139	sing through the tree-tops in dark syllables,
10.140	and fling faint murmur on the far-off sea,
10.141	to tell some pilot of to-morrow's storm.
10.142	Then Jupiter omnipotent, whose hands
10.143	have governance supreme, began reply;
10.144	deep silence at his word Olympus knew,
10.145	Earth's utmost cavern shook; the realms of light
10.146	were silent; the mild zephyrs breathed no more,
10.147	and perfect calm o'erspread the levelled sea.
10.148	“Give ear, ye gods, and in your hearts record
10.149	my mandate and decree. Fate yet allows
10.150	no peace 'twixt Troy and Italy, nor bids
10.151	your quarrel end. Therefore, what Chance this day
10.152	to either foe shall bring, whatever hope
10.153	either may cherish,—the Rutulian cause
10.154	and Trojan have like favor in my eyes.
10.155	The destinies of Italy constrain
10.156	the siege; which for the fault of Troy fulfills
10.157	an oracle of woe. Yon Rutule host
10.158	I scatter not. But of his own attempt
10.159	let each the triumph and the burden bear;
10.160	for Jove is over all an equal King.
10.161	The Fates will find the way.” The god confirmed
10.162	his sentence by his Stygian brother's wave,
10.163	the shadowy flood and black, abysmal shore.
10.164	He nodded; at the bending of his brow
10.165	Olympus shook. It is the council's end.
10.166	Now from the golden throne uprises Jove;
10.167	the train of gods attend him to the doors.
10.168	Meanwhile at every gate the Rutule foe
10.169	urges the slaughter on, and closes round
10.170	the battlements with ring of flame. The host
10.171	of Trojans, prisoned in the palisades,
10.172	lies in strict siege and has no hope to fly.
10.173	In wretched plight they man the turrets tall,
10.174	to no avail, and with scant garrison
10.175	the ramparts crown. In foremost line of guard
10.176	are Asius Imbrasides, the twin
10.177	Assaraci, and Hicetaon's son
10.178	Thymoetes, and with Castor at his side
10.179	the veteran Thymbris; then the brothers both
10.180	of slain Sarpedon, and from Lycian steep
10.181	Clarus and Themon. With full-straining thews
10.182	lifting a rock, which was of some huge hill
10.183	no fragment small, Lyrnesian Acmon stood;
10.184	nor less than Clytius his sire he seemed,
10.185	nor Mnestheus his great brother. Some defend
10.186	the wall with javelins; some hurl down stones
